The Data Science Lifecycle
With a better idea of what data science is and the types of data commonly used in data science, you can explore the stages and processes that make up the data science lifecycle. Using a systematic, process-based approach like a lifecycle ensures that all relevant activities are performed and that the efforts of the data science team have a positive effect on the organization.

Lifecycle Stages
Like software development and other complex business initiatives, it can be helpful to identify a framework for the activities involved in completing an initiative. In many cases, a lifecycle can show progression towards the end result as well as the cyclical nature of such endeavors. As data science evolves and more companies get involved in the practice, no single lifecycle is recognized as superior to any of the others. For the purposes of this course, the data science lifecycle consists of the following stages:
1. Problem identification, including formulating questions that define a business problem and the business goals that can be achieved by solving the problem. Identification of potential data sources is also part of this stage.
2. Data collection.
3. Preprocessing, including data wrangling and munging.
4. Exploratory data analysis.
5. Modeling, including feature selection, training, and evaluation.
6. Model deployment.
7. Communication of results.


Figure 3-9: The data science lifecycle.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals | Topic C

84 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
As you review these stages, keep in mind that certain roles and activities will be involved in more than one phase. The stages that are relevant to most business professionals are likely to be those in which they have input and can provide feedback.
Examples of Data Science Lifecycles
As mentioned, no single data science lifecycle will be able to meet the needs of every organization. Here are a couple of examples of other lifecycles, most of which include similar stages:
• The Microsoft Team Data Science Process (TDSP) lifecycle:
1. Business understanding
2. Data acquisition and understanding
3. Modeling
4. Deployment
5. Customer acceptance
• The Data Science Process Alliance's general data science lifecycle:
1. Problem definition
2. Data investigation and cleaning
3. Minimal viable product/model
4. Deployment and enhancement
5. Data science operations
• The Cross-Industry Standard Process for Data Mining (CRISP-DM):
1. Business understanding
2. Data understanding
3. Data preparation
4. Modeling
5. Evaluation
6. Deployment

Problem Identification
The Problem Identification phase of the data science lifecycle encompasses the processes and activities that lay the foundation of the entire system. This phase requires domain knowledge to have any chance of success. You can think of this as the "business" portion of data science. Key parts of the Problem Identification phase include:
• Developing questions that define a business problem and the business goals that can be achieved by solving the problem. Determine the model targets, which are those business variables that the data analysis needs to predict. What kinds of information do you want to extract? Though some companies just amass a great volume of data and then see where it leads them, it can be more expedient to identify specific issues that you want to investigate.
• Identifying bounding or restrictive parameters such as priorities, specifications, and budgets.
• Establishing that you have the necessary personnel, technology, time, and data to attain the established goal. This also includes identifying plans to acquire resources, such as data acquisition, sourcing, and collection options.
A general process for problem identification might include the following steps:
1. Clearly state the problem to be solved.
2. Describe the potential value of solving the problem.
3. Identify stakeholders, required resources, and possible risks.
4. Record and communicate a project plan.
The amount of effort an organization expends in this phase cannot guarantee success, but it does establish a scope and provides the environment for the remaining phases.
Key roles in this phase include:
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 85
• Stakeholders such as C-level executives and managers, and possibly key personnel in affected departments.
• Data scientist.
• Data-team manager.

Tools
Common tools used in problem identification include:
• Organizational policies and procedures.
• Periodic company and departmental reports.
• Budgets and time frames.

Data Collection
The Data Collection phase of the data science lifecycle focuses on the collection, exploration, and profiling of data. The data acquisition and sourcing options elicited in the Problem Identification phase are implemented here.
Key activities in this phase include:
• Gathering data from identified sources. In some instances, data scientists are asked to work with existing datasets that have been collected in the course of other investigations. How that data is gathered and stored can limit the questions it can answer, and relevant data is not always immediately available. Some of the considerations surrounding data collection include:
• Sourcing, or selecting the right data. Internal sources include log data, transactional data, email messages, and departmental or enterprise databases. External sources include social media and multimedia files such as audio, photos, and videos.
• Identifying what collection methods to use. You can create online surveys, develop collection software, leverage open data, subscribe to data feeds, and work with companies that use similar sorts of data.
• Identifying how to store the data as it is collected. When massive amounts of data are in play, you will likely want to use a cloud-based storage solution that is highly scalable.
• Performing an initial investigation of the data to ensure it contains the information required to solve the business problem. Results of this activity include a description of the data and its structure, and confirmation of the relevance of the data. Data profiling is the process of examining, analyzing, and summarizing a dataset to determine the level of data quality of the dataset. When performed prior to preprocessing and cleaning, profiling can help to identify how much effort may be involved in those tasks.
Again, although expending effort in this phase cannot guarantee success, having high-quality, consistently formatted data provides the optimal input for your data science project.
Key roles in this phase include:
• Data scientist
• Data analyst
• Data-team manager
• Database administrator
Tools for Data Collection and Generation
Tools for collecting or generating data include:
• Business-to-consumer (B2C) applications, such as e-commerce and customer-loyalty apps.
• Sales and customer relationship management (CRM) systems such as Salesforce(r), Microsoft(r) Dynamics(r), and HubSpot(r).
• Web page traffic analyzers such as Google Analytics(tm) and Sprout Social(r). You can also garner this information from internal-server log files.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals | Topic C

86 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
• Web page scraping tools such as Beautiful Soup, Mozenda, and Octoparse.
Tools for Data Storage
Data storage tools can be local, cloud based, or a combination of both:
• Local storage is designed to hold structured data. Common examples include:
• Traditional relational database management systems (RDBMSs), such as Microsoft SQL Server(r) databases, Oracle(r) databases, SQL databases, and MySQL(r) databases. Traditional storage is present in most enterprises and can serve as a starting point, but once you reach a certain volume of data, the performance is affected. At that point (if you have not already), you will need to investigate more scalable solutions.
• Data warehouses. Unlike RDBMSs, which are designed to optimize the update of transactional data, data warehouses are optimized to support data aggregation.
• Data lakes. While a data warehouse is likely to hold databases that contain bounded information (like sales data or inventory data), a data lake can hold many categories of data in a central repository.
• Cloud-based storage is inherently scalable and well suited to large, diverse datasets. Common examples include:
• Amazon Web Services (AWS) technologies such as Amazon Redshift(r), AWS Lake Formation(tm), and Simple Storage Service (S3(r)) buckets.
• Google Cloud Platform(tm) technologies such as Cloud Storage(tm), Cloud SQL(tm), and Cloud Datastore(tm).
• Microsoft Azure(r) technologies such as Blob Storage(tm) and Data Lake Storage(tm).
• Hybrid storage, which provides access to local and cloud storage to provide flexibility, security, and performance in a cost-effective manner. Common examples include:
• Apache Hadoop(r), a free, open-source framework for storing and managing massive datasets.
• OpenStack(r) Swift, an open-source object-storage system designed to store unstructured data.
• Apache Cassandra(r), a distributed database designed to store structured data across multiple server locations.
• Cloud-service providers like AWS, Google, and Azure also support hybrid storage.

Preprocessing
Getting data from multiple sources means that it is probably not going to be formatted consistently and that there may be missing or duplicate information. Preprocessing is a series of activities that improve the quality of data to facilitate in-depth analysis. Common preprocessing tasks include:
• Data wrangling (or data munging) is the process of transforming raw data into a format that is better suited to analysis.
• Data cleaning refers to the manipulation of data to remove inconsistencies and address missing data points.
Related to preprocessing is loading data into a data sandbox or data workbench, which is a scalable development platform used to enable collaboration and exploration of large datasets. This is sometimes referred to as data integration. Techniques for loading data include extract, transform, and load (ETL), which pulls data from one or more sources, cleaning and formatting it before loading it into the sandbox.
The results of ETL often provide insight into the data model. A data model is a description of the relationships among various types of information. It organizes data elements and establishes standards for how the elements interrelate. Data models can be conceptual, physical, or logical:
• A conceptual model describes a high-level view of data. This is typically equivalent to the end user's view. For example, you might identify sources such as Salesforce or Oracle databases.
• A physical model is a schema or framework for how data is stored in a database. Some questions relevant to physical models include:
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 87
• How will you access the data?
• Will you need APIs developed?
• If you will use ETL, which fields should be accessed?
• What permissions are required for accessing various data sources?
• A logical data model sits between the physical and conceptual levels and enables the logical representation of data to be separate from its physical storage. To accomplish this, you need to find the relationships between sources and the data they hold.
Key roles in this phase include:
• Data scientist
• Data analyst
• Data-team manager
• Database administrator
Data Cleaning Activities
Some general steps for cleaning data include:
• Remove extra spaces.
• Find and address blank cells.
• Convert numbers stored as text into numeric format.
• Convert dates and times to a consistent format.
• Perform data matching (John Q. Public, John Public, and Jack Public might all refer to the same individual, even though there are separate records under each name).
• Remove duplicates.
• Remove outliers (those values that fall far from the rest of the data).
• Highlight and correct errors.
• Change text casing to be consistent.
• Check for and correct all spelling errors.
• Remove all formatting.
Other Data Loading Techniques
Although ETL is quite commonly used in data science projects, you might hear other techniques referred to, such as ELT or even ETLT.
• Extract, load, and transform (ELT) pulls data from one or more sources, cleaning and formatting it after it has been loaded into the sandbox.
• Extract, transform, load, and transform (ETLT) pulls data from one or more sources, cleaning and formatting it both before and after it has been loaded into the sandbox. The first transform cycle is often implemented to eliminate noise from the data.
Tools
Common data preprocessing tools include:
• Microsoft Excel. While Excel itself does not scale to enterprise-level data wrangling or cleaning, its vast presence on business computers makes it a common first choice for data cleaning tasks.
• R, which is a data-analysis programming language that supports statistical analysis, predictive modeling, and data visualization.
• Python, which is a high-level programming language that supports data manipulation and repetitive tasks.
• MATLAB, a proprietary programming language developed by The MathWorks(r), Inc. that enables programmers to manipulate matrices, plot functions and data, implement algorithms, and other tasks.
• SAS, a statistical software-application suite developed by SAS Institute(r) for managing and analyzing data.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals | Topic C

88 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	

• IBM(r) SPSS, a software platform that provides advanced statistical analysis, a library of ML algorithms, and other features. Two of the main applications in the suite are SPSS Statistics(tm) and SPSS Modeler(tm), which respectively provide top-down and bottom-up analysis.
• Apache Spark(tm), an open-source distributed cluster-computing framework that supports big data processing, as well as ML, visualization, and other data science-related computing tasks.
• Informatica(r) PowerCenter(r), an ETL tool used for building data warehouses.
• RapidMiner(r), an integrated environment for data science tasks such as data preparation, ML, and predictive analytics.

Exploratory Data Analysis
Exploratory data analysis (EDA) is a form of analysis that examines datasets, summarizes their main characteristics, and provides the summary information in a visual format. Characterized by using statistical functions such as averages, means, and medians, EDA provides initial insights into your data by:
• Calculating summary statistics.
• Identifying dependent and independent variables or features.
• Analyzing key features of data to work on.
• Defining the spread of data and identifying outliers.
Identifying relationships between variables, by performing some initial analytics to better understand the patterns inherent in the data, helps you establish a basis for the algorithms to be implemented.
For instance, aggregation is the process of gathering and summarizing data in a summarized report.
For thorough data analysis, different plots can be used to visualize the data for better understanding for everyone. Data scientists explore the distribution of data inside distinct variables graphically by the usage of bar graphs. Relations between distinct aspects can be captured via graphical representations like scatter plots and heat maps.
EDA and preprocessing can inform each other. For example, information you discover during EDA may require additional preprocessing to address outliers or inconsistent data types. EDA can also uncover features that are either required or unnecessary for modeling. Performing another round of preprocessing can ensure the most important features are selected. Many data teams find that including one or more iterative cycles of preprocessing and EDA result in a better model and more accurate predictions.
Key roles in this phase include:
• Data scientist
• Data analyst
Tools
Common tools used for EDA include:
• Applications like Tableau(r) and Power BI(r)
• Python
• R
• MATLAB
• SAS
• IBM SPSS
• Apache Spark(tm)
• RapidMiner



Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 89
Modeling
The Modeling phase of the data science lifecycle is where you develop a machine learning model that will perform the analytics required to solve the business problem. ML models consist of one or more algorithms and the model data. Algorithms are mathematical procedures or techniques that, when converted into code, enable a computer to identify patterns, make predictions, or perform tasks without explicit instructions. Activities in this phase include:
• Feature engineering, which is the process of using domain knowledge, mathematics, and statistics to manipulate a dataset to facilitate the training of an ML model. It is imperative that the most relevant variables are used as input for your ML model so that it can successfully predict outcomes.
• Model training is the process of feeding a small set of data (training data) into an ML algorithm, evaluating the results of the algorithm, and optimizing the algorithm for performance. Model training is an iterative process and is often performed on several algorithms until one or more algorithms attain sufficient performance levels.
• Model validation occurs after training and confirms that a model actually achieves the intended purpose. During validation, a different set of data (testing data) is fed into an algorithm, and input, calculations, and output are all tested for accuracy.


Note: With several types of models involved, things can get confusing. Remember, data models represent the structure of data, ML models learn from data and make predictions, and statistical models infer information about a population based on sample data.Key roles in this phase include:
• Data scientist
• Data architect
• Database administrator
• Data engineer
• ML engineer
• Data-team manager
Tools
Common modeling tools include:
• Microsoft(r) Analysis Services, which is available for SQL Server(r), Power BI(r), and Azure(r).
• R
• Python(r)
• Jupyter(r) Notebooks, which is an open-source web application that enables you to create and share documents containing live code, equations, visualizations, and narrative text. It is used for data cleaning and transformation, numerical simulation, statistical modeling, data visualization, and machine learning.
• SAS/ACCESS(r), which is software designed to integrate SAS and third-party relational databases.

Model Deployment
Once the model is built, trained, and validated, you can deploy it. The Model Deployment phase includes the following activities:
• Operationalizing, or moving the data and the model out of the sandbox and into a live environment. The goal is to gather repeatable, scalable, and flawless results from data. Sandbox testing can help with the first and third part of the goal, but historically the greatest challenge has been scalability. Identifying tools that can automate the production of APIs or executable packages can significantly reduce the time and effort it takes to operationalize.


Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

90 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
• Monitoring and analyzing the data periodically to ensure that the model is generating the expected results. When acceptable bounds are exceeded, the team can revisit earlier stages in the lifecycle to address any required adjustments.
Key roles in this phase include:
• Data engineer
• Database administrator
• Software engineer
• Implementation engineer

Communication of Results
To ensure that you have achieved the goals set in the Problem Identification phase, you should gather the results of the initiative and communicate them to the stakeholders. That is the primary goal of the Communication of Results phase. Activities in this phase include:
• Identifying the key findings that were documented in each stage of the process.
• Creating visual representations of the insights you have gained.
• Packaging the findings and visualizations into reports and other assets that all stakeholders can understand and use.
Key roles in this phase include:
• Stakeholders such as C-level executives and managers, and possibly key personnel in affected departments.
• Data scientist.
• Data-team manager.
Tools
Common tools for communicating results include:
• Microsoft Excel
• Tableau

Lesson 3: Data Science Fundamentals |

Functions of Data Science in Business
Lesson Time: 1 hour




Lesson Introduction
Business professionals bring vital domain knowledge to every data science project. It is imperative that companies expend money and effort towards those projects that will offer the most beneficial results. Even with this consideration, there are a few use cases that almost any company might try to enhance by using data science. In this lesson, you will identify functions of data science in business.

Lesson Objectives
In this lesson, you will:
• Improve customer experience (CX).
• Improve marketing efforts.
• Optimize organizational and transactional security.
• Enhance operational practices.














Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

94 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC A
Improve Customer Service
Establishing and maintaining customer satisfaction is essential in most businesses, especially those that rely on repeat business to maximize their profitability. In this topic, you will review ways that data science can improve customer experience.

Customer Experience (CX)
Customer experience (CX) is the overall perception of a company based on customers' interactions with that company. Based on the internal, subjective responses of individuals, CX encompasses consumer reactions from each stage of the customer journey-enquiry, comparison, purchase, installation and use, and loyalty. CX is highly individualized in the details, but it is generally accepted that improving CX is a benefit to any business. When you add data science to the mix, several strategies for improving CX emerge as possible use cases:
• Personalization
• Sentiment analysis
• Recommender systems
• Self-service support
• Chatbots and virtual assistants

Personalized CX
The effort of personalizing CX and providing a specific set of interactions and services that is tailored to each individual consumer often helps customers feel valued, which in turn can lead to increased loyalty and the potential for repeat business. Predicting which services and interactions each customer will react positively to depends not only on the collection of customer data, but also on the correct interpretation of that data. This is where data science comes into play.
Often, different types of data are leveraged to provide personalization. Each stage of the customer journey enables a company to gather information from their customers, so personalization can begin almost immediately.
• Initial enquiry. At the start of the customer journey, consider providing more than one "front door" to potential customers. Some people appreciate a self-service approach, while others prefer more human interaction right from the start. Whether a customer's initial experience with your company is through direct contact with a sales representative or via your website or other online marketing materials, be sure to collect basic contact information (name, email, phone number, etc.) and at least a general idea of what the customer is interested in. Whenever possible, try to collect the same types of information no matter the source of the initial enquiry. In addition, consider following up the initial contact with a short survey to enable customers to provide more specific information about whatever it is they are seeking, as well as feedback about their impressions of the company so far.
• Comparison: When customers look for alternatives to past purchases, you want to position your company and its products and services as the most viable alternative. This stage can be considered a "conversation" between your company and the customer, with your goal being to encourage the customer to make a purchase. Gathering information from the customer about past experiences and current expectations enables you to tailor the information you share to provide enough information to facilitate decision making, but not so much information that potential customers are overwhelmed with unnecessary information. You can also leverage demographic data, browsing history, and purchase history to create different messages for different population segments. Another possibility is to create personalized offers and content based on the information gathered so far.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 95
• Purchase: Your main goal at this stage is to make the purchasing process as effortless as possible for each customer. For online and offline purchases, collect basic invoice data such as the specifications (such as quantity, size, and so forth) for each product, billing details, and shipping information. Offer the purchaser membership in existing rewards programs. Consider conducting another survey so that customers can provide feedback about the purchasing process.
• Implementation and use: After a sale, you will want to follow up with the customer to ensure that they are satisfied with the products and services that they have purchased. Elicit any areas of confusion or other trouble spots, and refer specifically to those when you respond to the customer. Ask customers to rate the product or service according to how closely it meets their needs, and ask for suggestions on how to improve the process.
• Loyalty: Your ultimate goal is to create and foster long-term relationships with your customers. Ensure that customer service personnel are trained to rapidly respond to questions and concerns, and to verify that customer concerns have been adequately addressed. Consider implementing tiered loyalty programs that offer different incentives for future purchases.

Figure 4-1: Try to collect data at every stage of the customer journey.
Sentiment Analysis
Sentiment analysis is a process that determines the emotional tone of textual data, often grouping results in positive, negative, and neutral categories. Sources for the textual data commonly encountered in sentiment analysis include emails, customer-support chat transcripts, online reviews, and other comments made on social medial platforms. The technologies used for sentiment analysis include machine learning (ML), statistics, and natural language processing (NLP), which is a form of artificial intelligence (AI) that enables computers to comprehend, generate, and respond to human language.
Sentiment analysis can provide insight into your customers' mindsets and uncover possible ways to increase customer satisfaction.


Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic A

96 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Recommender Systems
A recommender system is a form of machine learning that suggests potential items of interest based on a customer's profile and search history. By comparing this data with metadata about available inventory, a recommender system can suggest similar products and services that the customer may find relevant.
For example, if you search an online bookstore for a certain book title, you might receive recommendations such as additional books by the same author or in the same genre. Other online stores can suggest companion products to facilitate the use of items in your shopping cart, like the correct batteries for a battery-operated item you want to buy. Finally, if you are shopping for a certain type of clothing and you have viewed several options before making a selection, you might be shown other items in similar styles or colors. Each of these examples supports the company goal of making sales while driving customer satisfaction levels.

Self-Service Support
For customers, the availability of self-service support enables them to receive assistance when they need it-days, nights, or weekends-without the need for a company to hire customer service representatives to work around the clock. Self-service support is particularly beneficial for resolving basic issues, such as lost passwords, quickly and efficiently. Options for implementing self-service support include:
• Chatbots
• Knowledge bases and other documentation
• Online forums and communities
As convenient as self-service support can be, it cannot meet the needs of all customers. When more complex issues arise, or when the customer prefers human interaction to solve a problem, customer service and support personnel can provide extra guidance to help customers feel confident about the products and services they use.
So, how does the use of data science relate to self-service support efforts? You can collect data about support calls and analyze it to determine the most commonly experienced issues or questions, and to classify the issues or questions as involving basic or advanced support. You could then create frequently asked questions (FAQs) content to address the top 10 basic issues or questions.

Chatbots and Virtual Assistants
Perhaps the ultimate tool to personalize CX is the use of generative AI, which is a deep learning application of AI that is used to generate content such as text, audio, images, and video, in a way that appears convincingly humanlike. Popular implementations include chatbots and virtual assistants. These are similar, and may be used interchangeably in some instances, but they do have different roles and purposes.
• A chatbot is a program or feature that is designed to simulate human conversation through text or voice messages. Early chatbots used forms of pattern matching to interpret user messages, while more modern chatbots leverage AI (in particular, machine learning). A few of the functions performed by chatbots include providing instructions to complete a task, gathering and sharing product or contact details, setting calendar appointments, and integrating with a knowledge base or self-service portal. Often deployed on websites, within apps, or on messaging platforms, chatbots use a conversational interface similar to standard text messaging to communicate with users. Popular chatbots that you may have experience with include the CNN(r) chatbot on Facebook(r) Messenger, Spotify(r), and OpenAI(r) ChatGPT(r). The primary goal of a chatbot is to provide customer support.


Note: The first chatbot was developed in 1966 by Joseph Weizenbaum, professor emeritus at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT).Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 97
• A virtual assistant is a software-based agent designed to assist users with performing simple
tasks. Examples of tasks include checking calendar appointments, setting alarms, initiating phone or video calls, typing text messages, automating workflows, providing reminders, and offering directions. Virtual assistants do not need to use an interface to communicate with users, and they are commonly built into devices or appliances. Popular virtual assistants include Apple(r) Siri(r), Amazon(r) Alexa(r), Microsoft(r) Cortana(r), and Google(r) Assistant(tm). The primary goal of a virtual assistant is to help users conduct business or personal activities.
If your company is looking to implement AI-based technology to improve CX, consider using chatbots as opposed to virtual assistants.




































Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Consider the following discussion questions to help review your understanding of methods for

1. Customers who use the GCE online store and other online resources have been experiencing substantial wait times to speak with customer service agents. This has led to some dissatisfaction and less-than-optimal online reviews.

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 99
TOPIC B
Improve Marketing Efforts
The primary goal of any marketing strategy is to maximize revenue while minimizing customer acquisition costs. An accurate understanding of the target audience is crucial, and it should be no surprise that data science can help. In this topic, you will identify how data science can improve marketing efforts.
Audience Segmentation
It's unlikely that a single marketing campaign can appeal to everyone. Things that appeal to parents in their 20s and 30s are probably different from the things that a teenager finds interesting.
Understanding these differences enables you to isolate the market into categories and offer specific selling points to each category. This makes your campaigns more precise because you can focus on serving smaller, more refined segments. Audience segmentation, sometimes referred to as customer segmentation or market segmentation, is the process of dividing individuals into groups based on common characteristics. Target markets can be divided in numerous ways, but a common approach is to use four main types of audience segmentation:
• Demographic segmentation divides an audience by variables such as age, gender, education level, occupation, and income.
• Geographic segmentation divides an audience by previously determined geographical boundaries.
• Psychographic segmentation divides an audience by intrinsic traits such as values, personalities, interests, attitudes, motivators, and opinions.
• Behavioral segmentation divides an audience by how they make decisions and work through the purchasing process.

Targeted Advertising
One of the goals of audience segmentation is to provide targeted advertising to consumers. Focusing on specific traits, preferences, and interests enables you to differentiate the messages sent to potential and existing customers. For this type of advertising, the following data sources are commonly used:
• Web tracking, most often in the form of third-party cookies.
• Location tracking, through the use of technology like GPS signals, cellular triangulation, and Bluetooth(r) connectivity.
• Cross-device tracking, which uses data from multiple devices to collect user data and preferences.
While studies show that well over half of consumers find targeted advertising appealing, others may be concerned with privacy and other ethical issues.

Campaign Optimization
As the name implies, campaign optimization is the process of increasing the effectiveness of marketing and advertising campaigns. The ultimate goal of any campaign is to maximize the return on investment (ROI) for the company, and data science can help.
Campaign optimization software such as Google Analytics(tm), WordStream(r), and others can identify and track metrics that signify the success of a campaign. They may even suggest ways to improve the metrics and therefore the effectiveness of the campaign. These metrics, often referred to as key performance indicators (KPIs), include:

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic B

100 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
• Return on ad spend (ROAS): Comparing the conversion rate to the amount spent on the campaign.
• Cost per click: Identifying the unit cost of the investment.
• Conversion rate: Identifying how many visitors were converted into customers.

Marketing Measurement Analysis
Similar to campaign optimization is the concept of marketing measurement analysis, which is the process of collecting, analyzing, and interpreting data to measure the effectiveness of marketing activities. While campaign optimization focuses on a particular campaign, marketing measurement analysis can also compare the effectiveness of different campaigns. Using information about the relative success of previous marketing activities can help drive the success of future campaigns.
Many organizations use a variety of metrics for this analysis. Some popular metrics include those described in the following table.

Reach	The number of people who are exposed to a marketing message. Frequency	The number of instances a person is exposed to a marketing message. Engagement	The level of interaction with a marketing message.
Conversion	The number of people who react to a marketing message as was intended by the organization.
ROI	The amount of money generated by a marketing campaign divided by the amount of money spent on the campaign.

Common tools for collecting and analyzing marketing data include web, social media, and email analytics tools.

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic B

102 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC C
Optimize Organizational and Transactional Security
In addition to the customer-facing issues discussed so far, most businesses have immense interest in ensuring the security of their systems and data. Not surprisingly, data science can help with security issues, too. In this topic, you will identify ways that data science can help optimize organizational and transactional security.

Fraud Detection
Fraud detection is an area where data science can benefit a business. Banking organizations analyze transactional data and can flag anomalous activity that is not overtly fraudulent, but AI has determined is possibly related to fraud, such as several consecutive small transactions followed by a larger transaction. Once the flag has been raised, the bank can contact the account holder to verify the transactions and take corrective action.

Minimization of Loan Defaults
Financial institutions can use data science techniques to more accurately predict the likelihood that a potential borrower will default on a loan. For instance, analyzing historical data in the form of past loan applications and whether or not the loans were paid in full can provide some insight as to the types of loans that are most likely to go into default. The organization may decide to assign weights to various loan types and then, when an individual applies for a particular type of loan, that weighting can be factored into the overall determination of risk. Other factors that could be considered include:
• The applicant's identifying information (name, taxpayer identification number, etc.).
• The applicant's financial history.
• The applicant's current work status and financial standing.
• The estimated value of the property to be acquired.
• The loan amount.

Reduction of Intellectual Property Theft
Intellectual property (IP) is a category of property that includes intangible creations of human intellect, such as literary and artistic works, inventions, designs, and so forth. Probably the best-known examples of IP are patents, copyrights, trademarks, and trade secrets. As you can imagine, IP theft results in adversity for the victim, including losing a competitive edge, damage to organizational reputation, slow or stagnant growth, and losing customer trust.
There are numerous best practices to prevent or reduce the impact of IP theft, many of which relate to data security. For example, having systems in place to continuously monitor user activity with regard to IP data provides companies with a warning mechanism that supports investigation in real time. So, how can data science help to reduce IP theft? Extending the concept of user monitoring, user and entity behavior analysis (UEBA) uses AI to establish a baseline of user behavior. If the user performs anomalous activities, security personnel can be alerted in real time. Another example is seeding a honey trap-using AI and NLP to create and publish false information intended to lure and mislead adversaries.


Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 103
Risk Identification and Mitigation
The previous sections identify several specific types of organizational risk and how data science can help address these risks. On a more general note, data science can assist in identifying and mitigating many forms of operational and strategic risks. Operational risk includes disruption of day-to-day business processes, while strategic risk arises when a company cannot react to market conditions quickly enough. Both of these types of risk can be lessened somewhat by the implementation of data science.
For example, one form of operational risk is failure of mission-critical equipment. Predictive modeling could be used to anticipate component failure and allow for the components to be replaced on a preventive maintenance schedule. The schedule itself might be derived from data about number and size of orders, any work that is scheduled for other equipment, and disaster recovery data regarding mean time to failure (MTTF) and mean time to repair (MTTR).
Data science is just as effective at mitigating strategic risk. Prescriptive modeling can not only identify changing customer requirements or industry trends, but it can also offer suggestions for how to adapt to those changes.

Cybersecurity Considerations
The use of data science to strengthen cybersecurity measures is a key component of most of the popular cybersecurity strategies currently in use. Some examples of this phenomenon include:
• The first stage of any cyber attack is likely to be reconnaissance, where the attacker gathers information about potential targets. Network traffic analysis (NTA) is the process of capturing and analyzing network data packets to gain insights into the behavior, performance, and security of a computer network. It involves examining the patterns, protocols, and volume of data flowing through a network to understand how devices and systems communicate with each other. Companies can use NTA devices to identify unusual behavior, alert cybersecurity personnel to the situation, and possibly even block the continuation of the anomalous activity.
• Monitoring user behavior to identify patterns and anomalies can also uncover possible vulnerabilities. User and entity behavior analytics is a cybersecurity tool that uses algorithms and ML to detect anomalies in the behavior of the users in a corporate network, as well as the routers, servers, and endpoints in that network. For instance, if user Gio normally downloads a few small files over the space of a week, but starts downloading multiple large files every day, the UEBA system can flag the activity and alert security personnel of a potential threat.
• Analyzing historical data to identify and prioritize cybersecurity risks can help organizations focus their resources on protecting against the most critical threats.
• Implementing machine learning and NLP to analyze online content for potential cybersecurity risks can help organizations respond to cyber attacks in real time.

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic C

TOPIC D

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 105

Enhance Operational Practices
The final category of business uses discussed in this lesson involves using data science practices for the optimization of common day-to-day tasks. In this topic, you will identify ways that data science can help enhance operational practices.

System or Component Failure
A common hallmark of reliability for a business is system availability, which is expressed as a percentage, such as 99.999%. Many organizations maintain a high level of availability by employing predictive maintenance, which is the practice of monitoring the overall performance and condition of specific equipment during regular operations to avoid unplanned downtime.
Many companies use predictive analytics to estimate the probability of system or component failure based on historical information about past failures. This information enables these companies to schedule planned downtime to replace components or otherwise ensure that systems do not fail.
For examples of predictive maintenance, visit: https://blog.brewerscience.com/six-examples-of-predictive-maintenance.

Sales Forecasting
Another common use for data science is creating and refining sales forecasts. By analyzing historical data, identifying trends and patterns, and applying machine learning models, businesses can make better decisions about staffing, inventory, marketing, and pricing. Forecasting can be short term or long term, identifying monthly, quarterly, and annual benchmarks. Common methods for sales forecasting include:
• Time-series forecasting uses data from previous periods (like monthly or quarterly sales) and estimates future outcomes.
• Regression forecasting uses a combination of historical data and linear regression to analyze how numerous factors change (such as market condition and economic trends) and affect sales efforts over time.
• Historical forecasting analyzes past sales performance to identify trends and other patterns and uses that information to predict future sales.
• Opportunity-stage forecasting use data from current transactions (those within a production or fulfilment pipeline) and other factors to anticipate customers' future needs.

Dynamic Pricing
Dynamic pricing is the process of adjusting the price of goods or services according to customer behavior or changes in the market. A well-known example of dynamic pricing is the fluctuation of airline ticket prices, where factors such as seat type, number of available seats, and the number of days until the flight takes place are considered when a ticket-price quote is requested.

Customer Churn
Customer churn is the tendency of customers to cancel subscriptions to a service, or otherwise cease their relationship with a business. Basically, you can think of customer churn as the opposite of customer retention. Although some churn occurs naturally, it is important to recognize increases in customer churn, the causes of the change, and actions that the company can take to minimize

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 4: Functions of Data Science in Business | Topic D

106 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
churn. Data science, and predictive analytics specifically, can help identify the factors that lead to customer churn, which may include:
• Negative customer experiences, such as the perceived low quality of products and services, lack of effective customer support, and pricing increases without added value.
• Changes in customer priorities, including changing economic situations or no longer needing a product or service.
• Switching to a competitor with better pricing or product selection.
Once the main causes are identified, the company can address them, possibly by improving customer service, offering discounts or loyalty rewards, and similar actions.
The type of data required to calculate and predict customer churn includes demographic and behavioral data as well as information about interactions with the company, such as beginning and end dates for a subscription, pricing data, and monthly recurring revenue (MRR). This data is analyzed to identify characteristics that are common to former customers, and the resulting information is compared to existing customer data to determine if there are any customers who fit the identified pattern. Those customers could then be contacted and offered an incentive to stay with the company.

Talent Acquisition
Recruiters, HR personnel, and other hiring managers have historically relied on perceptions and intuition to determine which candidate is the best fit for an open position, with understandably mixed results. Due to the virtual explosion of data science over the past several years, companies can now implement talent acquisition (TA) analytics to analyze recruitment data and candidate data to optimize the recruiting and hiring processes for the organization. Many HR/recruiting software packages include features such as automated candidate sourcing and tracking, résumé parsing, and email communication, all of which save humans from performing tedious and repetitive tasks related to the recruitment process.
Metrics that are useful in TA analytics include:
• Time to fill, or the number of calendar days between approval for posting the position and the hire date of the selected candidate.
• Time to hire, or the number of calendar days between a candidate applying for a position (or being contacted by a recruiter) and accepting an offer of employment.
• Offer acceptance rate, or the percentage of candidates who accepted offers out of the total number of candidates who received offers.
• Recruitment channel effectiveness, or the number of candidates obtained from various sources such as company websites, career search engines, social media platforms, and external recruitment agencies.
• Quality of hire, or the value a candidate brings to the company.

Transportation and Logistics
Some of the ways data science can help optimize the functions of transportation and logistics include:
• Dynamic route planning takes into consideration real-time data such as weather, traffic conditions, and active construction zones to provide the best possible route for deliveries.
• Demand prediction and dynamic pricing can enable companies to offer reduced rates to common delivery destinations.
• Warehouse management and optimization enables companies to monitor inventories and optimize the placement of items to facilitate handling and shipping.
• Operational capacity planning, or evaluating production resources to ensure that a manufacturer can meet product demand.

Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science
Lesson Time: 1 hour




Lesson Introduction
So far, you have explored data science fundamentals and identified numerous compelling business uses for data science. To put this information to work and transform your business, there are certain requirements you should meet to ensure that the transformation is smooth and successful. In this lesson, you will implement business requirements for data science.

Lesson Objectives
In this lesson, you will:
• Develop a data-centric organization.
• Develop an implementation strategy.
• Identify the impact of data science on business.
• Identify governance measures.












Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

112 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC A
Develop a Data-Centric Organization
The successful and sustainable transformation of a business into one that embraces the philosophies and benefits of using data science requires some preparation at the organizational level. In this topic, you will examine the requirements for developing a data-centric organization.

What Is a Data-Centric Organization?
You may have heard the terms "data driven" and "data centric" used interchangeably, but there are some notable differences between the two. You can think of data driven as the mindset that supports making strategic decisions based on data, while data centric goes further to encompass the concept and the practice of positioning data as a core asset of the organization.
A data-centric organization is one where management and employees not only believe that data analytics are an integral component of the overall business strategy, but also implements a framework in which data is stored independently of the applications and tools that rely on it. The intent of a data-centric organization is to generate relevant information and use it in a collaborative manner to achieve successful business outcomes.

Figure 5-1: Data-centric organizations place data at the core of the organization.

Challenges Associated with Building a Data-Centric Organization
As you might expect, building a data-centric organization is a complex undertaking that can provide significant rewards when proper planning is incorporated into the process. A few of the common challenges include:
• Data silos. Data silos occur when data is stored in separate systems that cannot communicate with each other. This makes it difficult to access and analyze data from different parts of the organization. Migrating data from silos to central data storage provides the core of the data-centric environment.
• Data quality. Data quality is essential for any data-driven organization; however, it can be difficult to ensure that data is accurate, complete, and up to date. This is especially true when

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 113
data is collected from multiple sources or when it is manually entered. Include sufficient time and resources to ensure that the consolidated data is clean and free of errors.
• Data governance. Data governance is the set of policies and procedures that govern how data is collected, stored, and used. It is essential for ensuring the security and privacy of data, as well as for ensuring that data is used ethically, but data governance initiatives can be complex and time consuming to implement. Organizations need to ensure that they are compliant with all applicable data privacy and security regulations. This can be a complex and evolving area of law, and organizations need to stay up to date on the latest requirements.
• Trust. Several levels of trust are crucial to ensuring the success of the data-centric organization.
• Organizational management and IT must establish and maintain trust in each other and in the benefits of the changes being implemented.
• Employees and customers need to trust that their data is being used responsibly and ethically. If people do not trust the organization with their data, they will be less likely to share it. This can make it difficult to collect the data that is needed to make informed decisions.
• Changing roles and responsibilities. As part of the consolidation of data that is distributed throughout an organization, the roles and responsibilities of individuals who work with the data must also change.
Some organizations that have worked through the process to become data centric include:
• The Boeing(r) Company, a multinational manufacturer of aerospace products.
• JPMorgan Chase(tm) & Co., a multinational financial services company.
• Domino's(r), a multinational restaurant chain.

Organizational Preparation
Preparing a company to be a successful data-centric organization involves the following:
• Obtaining management buy-in. As with any major endeavor that results in fundamental change to an organization, the backing and support of upper management is required. Having one or more data evangelists in a leadership position can help ensure that data quality and management issues will be addressed. This can also ensure that lines of communication between all levels of the organization remain open and fruitful.
• Achieving alignment with organizational objectives and values.
• Make sure you understand the overall business objectives of the organization, then examine the existing data infrastructure and its relationship to those objectives. Bringing these into alignment provides a data strategy that supports using and interpreting data as a central asset of the organization.
• Identify organizational resources (people, equipment, and processes) that may be required to achieve these goals. Compare this inventory with what the organization presently has in place to identify gaps or the need for updating or replacing resources.
• Consider the culture of the organization. What parts of the culture lend themselves well to a data-centric approach? Characteristics like design thinking, objectivity, clear channels of communication, and a focus on learning and professional development all help contribute to a data-centric mindset. Determine what enhancements are required to position the company for success.
• Identifying potential contributors. Although the overall initiative will affect everyone at some point, there are several individuals or roles that can greatly assist the transformation process. Some organizations create specific data teams, while others prefer to use an ad hoc approach. The common thread is that potential contributors have a clear understanding of the vision behind creating a data-centric organization, including the anticipated benefits to the organization. Commonly recognized stakeholders include leaders and other decision makers, data owners, data science practitioners, and data consumers. Each organization will have varying criteria for selecting the appropriate personnel to drive the necessary changes.
• Preparing teams to succeed. Ensuring a successful business transformation is impossible without a concerted effort to provide teams with the tools they need to be successful.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic A

114 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Team Preparation
As part of organizational preparation, the company should have performed a resource assessment to identify equipment, processes, and personnel needed to achieve its goals. To fulfill the requirements for personnel resources, most companies will use a combination of training existing employees and adding new personnel, either as employees or through the use of contractors, with the goal of having a talent ecosystem that has the required level of data literacy, which is the ability to read, analyze, communicate, and reason with data.
• Training. Transforming to a data-centric organization requires some form of training for all existing employees. After all, processes, tools, and tasks are likely to change, and each employee needs to be aware of how their roles and responsibilities are affected by the transformation. Some job roles may be eliminated, others augmented, and still others created from scratch, so the people in those roles may need upskilling-training that gives employees the skills and knowledge to help them perform their jobs more effectively.
• Talent recruiting and acquisition. It's quite likely that there will be new or existing job roles that need to be filled by new personnel. Deciding whether to add full-time employees, use contractor or other third-party talent, or implement some combination of the two will obviously depend on each organization's unique needs.
• Combining roles. Many organizations find that combining certain job roles provides an optimal solution. Obviously, a large enterprise is in a better position to hire more experienced professionals than a small or medium business is, but the need remains the same. In many cases, companies will combine job roles and ask team members to extend themselves to perform tasks that might otherwise be performed by another.

TOPIC B
Develop an Implementation Strategy
Recall the data science lifecycle discussed earlier in the course. As you might expect, there are several considerations to be taken into account when this lifecycle is operationalized in the workplace. In this topic, you will develop an implementation strategy.

Selection of Business Cases for Implementation
Earlier in the course, you discovered numerous business cases for using data science, including audience segmentation, recommender systems, and fraud detection. It could be very disruptive to attempt to implement these use cases all at once. For a successful implementation, each organization should examine the possible use cases to determine things like:
• Which use cases will provide the most benefit to the overall organization.
• Which use cases can be implemented at a relatively low cost.
• Which use cases can be implemented in a relatively short time period.
Once this information has been obtained, the company can select the order of implementation that is best suited to its needs. You may also find that some of the use cases will not provide enough benefit to outweigh the costs associated with implementing them. For example, if a particular project requires that the company spend a large amount of capital for new equipment but the anticipated benefits of implementation are not mission critical, it may be advantageous to delay the project, or even drop it altogether.

Implementation Investments
As you plan for the implementation of data science projects, it's crucial to consider the types of investments each project will need, such as:
• People. Your data team needs the skills and experience to collect, clean, analyze, interpret, and communicate data and insights. In most cases, investment in staffing and training will be substantial.
• Technology. Your organization needs certain tools and physical resources to ensure the success of its data science initiatives. These tools and resources include software tools for collecting, cleaning, analyzing, and visualizing data, and hardware resources such as servers, storage, and networking.
• Data. Access to large amounts of high-quality data is necessary for every data science project. Data can come from a combination of internal and external sources, with varying levels of quality, so investments in data can range from purchasing access to data to building a data pipeline that collects, cleans, stores, and moves data.
• Governance. Investing in the development of a solid data-governance program-one that ensures that data is secure, private, accurate, available, and usable-supports better decision making, improves cost controls, and contributes greatly to regulatory compliance.


Note: Data governance and regulatory compliance are discussed in depth later in this lesson.• Time. Organizations must be aware of the time factor for data science projects. Rushing to completion will likely provide less than optimal results, and can even result in project failure.



Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 117
Data Collection Considerations
Different issues affect the various stages of the data science lifecycle. Consider the following as you prepare for data collection activities:
• Make sure that relevant data is collected. This actually begins during the Problem Identification stage. A clear definition of what the organization wants to achieve can help identify what data needs to be collected.
• Identify options for collecting relevant data. Options include actions such as collecting data directly-by using surveys, interviews, and other instruments-or collecting and acquiring data from a third-party source such as public databases, government websites, and social media platforms.
• Identify and procure tools for generating synthetic data. In some cases, it is more expeditious to generate synthetic data by using tools like synthetic data generators and data augmentation tools. Using tools to generate synthetic data for use in data science initiatives eliminates the need to provide access to confidential or sensitive information and can help enhance datasets so that they better represent the population.
• Determine the best method for storing data. If you are using structured data, a relational database might be the optimal storage method. For a mix of data types, a data lake might be the best method. Make sure you also consider the benefits and drawbacks of using on-premises storage or cloud storage, or a combination of both.
• Identify how data from different sources will be combined. Initially, you might combine, or integrate, multiple datasets into a single dataset to get a comprehensive view of the data. If you find that an existing dataset should include more or different information, you can update or enrich the dataset by appending data to it. The appended data can be entire new records or new fields incorporated into the existing records.

Data Preparation Considerations
Consider the following as you prepare for data preparation activities such as preprocessing and exploratory data analysis (EDA):
• Preprocessing activities such as data wrangling and cleaning improve the quality of data to facilitate in-depth analysis. Identify and use tools that streamline the preprocessing of data.
• EDA activities such as examining datasets, summarizing main characteristics, and visually displaying summary information provide initial insight into the data and can facilitate the selection of algorithms and model types. Select and use data analysis tools that support each of these activities.

Modeling and Deployment Considerations
Consider the following as you prepare for modeling and deployment activities such as feature engineering, model training and validation, operationalization, and monitoring:
• Select algorithms and machine learning (ML) models that identify patterns, make predictions, or perform tasks that support solving the identified business problem.
• After a model is selected, tune the model's hyperparameters (those parameters that control the learning process and that cannot be derived from the data) to optimize performance.
• During model testing, identify and use multiple algorithms to ensure the model provides acceptable results.
• Evaluate the model's performance and its results. Getting the wrong answer quickly is of little benefit.
• Avoid deploying a model before the performance and results of the model are acceptable.
• Verify that the deployed model provides repeatable, scalable, and flawless results from data.
• Monitor output data over time to ensure that the model continues to provide the expected results.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic B

118 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Results Communication Considerations
Consider the following as you prepare for activities related to communicating the results of a data science project:
• Identify the audience that will receive the results, and adjust your communications accordingly. Remember that jargon and high levels of technical detail may not be appropriate for everyone.
• Provide a clear, concise summary of the project and any limitations associated with it to provide context to the audience.
• Highlight and explain the key findings to ensure that the audience understands the results.
• Consider using visualizations such as tables and charts to illustrate your findings to promote audience understanding. Select a data visualization tool that enables you to create compelling visualizations.
• Combine the summary, key findings, and visualizations into reports and other assets for stakeholders.
• Encourage follow-up questions and feedback to ensure that your message was received as you intended, and to identify possible improvements to your communication skills.






























Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 119

Scenario
Consider the following discussion questions to help review your understanding of the issues
involved in developing an implementation strategy.

1. You are part of a cross-functional team that needs to brainstorm possible data science projects for implementation over the next two quarters. Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic B
