TOPIC C
Identify the Impact of Data Science on Business
Now that you have a clearer picture of the basics of data science and several examples of its implementation, you can examine factors that have a positive or negative impact on your business. This information is crucial to making informed and intelligent decisions on how and where to incorporate data science into your business practices. In this topic, you will identify the impact that data science can have on the business.

Effects on Overall Business Operations
In addition to the changes that are implemented in various pockets of the business, data science can have both positive and negative effects on overall business operations.
• Reputational impact.
Here are some common positive effects that data science can have on an organization's reputation:
• Improved decision making, which can lead to increased profits, improved customer service, and more effective risk management.
• Enhanced customer experience, which can result in increases in customer satisfaction and loyalty.
• Improved product development, which can lead to more sales and higher profits through the development of products and services that are projected to be more successful than their predecessors or the competition.
Here are some common negative effects that data science can have on an organization's reputation:
• Data breaches, which can result in financial losses, reputational damage, and legal problems.
• Perception of unethical practices such as discriminatory decision making or using datasets with known biases, which can cause unfair treatment of employees or customers, damage to the company's reputation, and legal problems.
• Impact on talent pool.
Here are some common positive effects that data science can have on an organization's talent pool:
• Attracting top talent due to the organization's adoption of data science practices and/or a data-centric operating philosophy. Companies that are willing to invest in the resources required for success often appear more attractive to data science professionals.
• Improvement of employee retention. Effective use of data science can provide staff with more challenging and satisfying job tasks, as well as plenty of learning and growth opportunities.
• Increased employee productivity. When data science is used to automate repetitive tasks, the employees who formerly performed those tasks are free to focus on more strategic tasks.
Here are some common negative effects that data science can have on an organization's talent pool:
• Skills gaps. The explosion of data science as a business tool has caused there to be a shortage of experienced practitioners. This could lead to companies competing to recruit those individuals with impressive skill sets.
• Prohibitive costs. Implementing data science projects can be costly, especially due to the efforts to acquire the best possible talent to the organization. When large capital expenditures are required, this can strain the implementation budget to make it more difficult to attract and retain talented employees.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 121
• Legal and regulatory compliance.
Here are some common positive effects that data science can have on an organization's compliance efforts:
• Improvements in compliance. Organizations that use data science as part of their risk identification and mitigation strategies often realize an increase in their compliance status as a direct result. Companies can also use data science practices to drive compliance management.
• Reduced costs. Some compliance tasks are relatively easy to automate through the use of data science, leading to lower staffing costs attributed to the company's compliance efforts.
Here are some common negative effects that data science can have on an organization's compliance efforts:
• As mentioned previously, data breaches can lead to legal repercussions.
• Unethical practices such as bias and discrimination are the target of many forms of legislation around the world. Each organization needs to be aware of which regulations apply so that it can implement practices that keep the organization in compliance with all applicable requirements.


Note: Legal and regulatory issues are covered in depth in the next topic.
Effects on Business Processes and Practices
Data science can also have an effect on various business processes and practices, including:
• Cost management efforts. Implementation of new or enhanced processes that support the use of data science can bring with it a large initial investment, especially if the organization is undergoing the change to data-centric principles. As with every other organizational initiative, optimizing the return on investment (ROI) for data science projects can be a concern. Even if a project succeeds in providing insights, could those insights have been achieved with more traditional analytic methods that required fewer resources?
• Management of infrastructure requirements. Managing complex infrastructure requirements can be daunting for the most seasoned professionals. However, with the help of data science, several types of efficiencies can be realized. For example, using data science to predict component failure or the need for more computing power and storage helps organization plan enhancements to meet future needs. Automation of management and optimization tasks helps ensure that the infrastructure operates at peak efficiency.
• Employee impact. Organizations that use data science should see varying levels of impact to their employees depending on job roles and other factors. In addition to the impacts on the overall talent pool, companies are advised to manage these changes in a way that provides employees with opportunities for upskilling and ways to increase their productivity.
• Organizational changes. For large-scale organizational changes like transforming to a data-centric organization, the effects of using data science can be widespread. However, increases in the quality of decision making, efficiency, innovation, and communication provide numerous benefits to help offset any potential negative effects.

Data Issues
It should be clearly apparent that implementing data science practices in a business will affect organizational data; however, a few notable data issues that are common in most organizations are described in the following table.




Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic C

122 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	

Data security	Data science projects should be designed and implemented with security in mind. Organizations need to protect not only the data, but also the analytics themselves, from attack, theft, or other malicious actions. The nature of data science makes it potentially vulnerable to compromise whether the project is on-premises, cloud based, or a hybrid project with components in both areas.
Data controls	Data controls are measures and practices that help mitigate data risks.
Common data controls include security measures to prevent unauthorized access; backup and recovery procedures to ensure the timely restoration of data that is lost for any reason; governance practices that describe how data is collected, used, and managed; and employee education to support the protection of data, including how to identify and report suspicious activity.
Data quality	The quality of data has an enormous impact on the success or failure of any data science initiative. If you think about it, the entire Preprocessing phase of the data science lifecycle deals with data quality. Also consider the effect of pooling data from different sources into a dataset. If your proprietary data is high quality, but you augment it with data of lesser quality, the quality of the entire dataset is degraded.
Data privacy	Data science can have profound effects on the protection of individuals' privacy. Datasets contain a vast amount of information (often from different sources), and any person or organization that controls the datasets also has control over the individual chunks of information.
Because this information is likely to contain personal information, privacy becomes a significant concern. The processing of data might also threaten privacy. Combining data from different sources may reveal personal characteristics or other information that was not meant to be shared.

As mentioned previously, developing and implementing a solid data-governance plan can help address each of these data issues.

AI-Related Risks
Risks related to the implementation of artificial intelligence (AI) systems include:
• Lack of formal governance for AI. When organizations don't adopt governance measures for AI systems, problems can arise with regard to biased models, privacy issues, security issues, and accountability issues. It is highly recommended that any organization looking to leverage AI in their business practices develop formal governance in the form of:
• Policies and procedures for the development, deployment, and use of AI systems.
• Employee training designed to teach the responsible use of AI systems.
• Monitoring and auditing requirements to ensure that AI systems are used safely and responsibly.
• Methods for ensuring transparency and explainability surrounding the collection of data, the use of data, and the possible risks inherent in using AI systems.


Note: Governance measures are covered in depth in the next topic.• Acquisition of third-party data and AI products. Using third-party data and AI systems means that you have very little control over important items such as data quality and provenance. This can lead to problems such as data bias, privacy issues, security vulnerabilities, and accountability issues. To counter these risks, perform a thorough review of the data and the
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 123
terms of the agreement between your organization and the vendor, and ensure that all applicable compliance requirements are met.
• Lack of domain expertise during creation and testing of AI systems. Domain expertise is an integral component in the creation and use of AI systems to provide data science capabilities. Without domain expertise, it is difficult to discern if training data is truly representative of real-world data or is even accurate. Be sure to include domain experts in the creation and testing of AI systems to avoid bias and inaccuracies, as well as security vulnerabilities.
• Use of black-box models and technologies. In the world of AI, a black-box model is one that is not transparent with regard to its decision-making processes, which leads to difficulty in understanding the reasoning behind the decisions the system makes. Without that understanding, organizations are at risk of incorporating bias into the model; experiencing unintended consequences that can have adverse effects on people, society, or the environment; and leaving the model vulnerable to security attacks. Whenever possible, use interpretable models that use a transparent decision-making process. If you must use a black-box model, consider implementing safeguards such as using multiple models or requiring human review and oversight of all decisions made by the model. Finally, make sure you communicate the fact that a black-box model is being used and explain the risks associated with its use.

Identify Governance Measures
So far, you have identified several strategies for implementing business requirements to support data science, including an introductory look at some ethical considerations and some legal and regulatory considerations. This topic expands on the introductory information to provide a more focused examination of these types of issues. In this topic, you will identify governance measures.

Ethical Considerations
Data ethics is a collection of moral principles that govern how organizations gather, protect, and use data. A fundamental goal of data ethics is to consider and protect the rights and interests of people and institutions outside of the organization. As a business decision maker, you won't necessarily be asked to take on the deep philosophical issues involved with data science. However, you are still responsible for ensuring that your data science projects follow general ethical principles. In some organizations, ethics have actually become a standard part of corporate board reporting.
This course highlights five major ethical principles in emerging technologies like data science:
• Privacy. As mentioned earlier, protecting the privacy of individuals, groups, and other entities who are represented by the data found in datasets is of particular concern.
• Accountability. Accountability refers to the practice of ensuring that people are held responsible for their actions, but when machine learning is used to implement data science, who is responsible for the insights produced by the model?
• Transparency and explainability. Transparency refers to the idea that people should be able to see into the inner workings of the technology, while explainability refers to the ability to understand how and why the technology works to arrive at a result. Probably the best example of transparency and explainability in data ethics is the black-box problem described earlier.
• Fairness and non-discrimination. The principles of fairness and non-discrimination protect people from being treated improperly because of who they are. They are primarily undermined by bias, which is a major issue in AI and ML. If the source data used to train a model contains bias, it's highly likely that any results generated by the model will also contain bias. Organizations need to take steps to ensure that training data contains minimal bias by addressing any disparities in representing specific groups, or minimizing the reinforcement of stereotypes within the dataset.
• Safety and security. While security is generally acknowledged to refer to protecting organizational assets, the idea of safety refers primarily to protecting human assets by minimizing the chances for physical harm or compromise of property. Building security controls into data science projects can help mitigate safety and security risks, but organizations need to understand that such techniques are not guaranteed to work in every situation.

Legal and Regulatory Considerations, Frameworks, and Guidelines
Numerous forms of laws and regulations exist to try to govern emerging technologies like data science practices. Some of the most well known include:
• The General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), a comprehensive regulation adopted by the EU to safeguard against data privacy violations and to provide regulations to ensure compliance by affected organizations.
• The Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI DSS) is a set of security standards designed to protect credit card data and prevent fraud.

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 5: Implementing Business Requirements for Data Science | Topic D

126 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
• The OECD Privacy Guidelines are a set of international guidelines for the protection of privacy. They were developed by the Organization for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) in 1980, and they have been revised several times since then. Although they are not legally binding, they are widely considered to be the international standard for privacy protection. They have been adopted by many countries, and they are often used as a reference point for national privacy laws and regulations.
• The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation Cross-Border Privacy Rules (APEC CBPR) is a set of voluntary privacy principles that aim to facilitate the safe and secure transfer of personal data across borders within the Asia-Pacific region.
With the rapid advancement of AI implementations, this is another arena where governments and other regulatory agencies are working to regulate the development and use of AI technologies. At the time of publication:
• China has adopted provisional regulations governing the use of generative AI.
• AI legislation is proposed in numerous countries, such as the EU's Artificial Intelligence Act and Canada's Artificial Intelligence and Data Act (AIDA).
Organizations must be aware of which laws and regulations apply to them. For instance:
• In the U.S., there is no comprehensive data privacy legislation, but several states have adopted laws to protect personal information.
• EU legislation, frameworks, and regulations like the GDPR can have a wider range than the geographical boundaries of the EU.
• According to the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development (UNCTAD), 79% of all countries have some sort of privacy or data protection legislation in place, and another 9% have draft legislation in the process of being ratified.
The landscape of legal and regulatory requirements is constantly changing. Some regulations are not put in place yet, but they are forthcoming. What does not affect your company today may do so in the future. It is crucial that your organization review new developments and respond accordingly to regulatory changes.

6 AI Fundamentals
Lesson Time: 1 hour, 15 minutes




Lesson Introduction
Before you explore the many ways in which artificial intelligence (AI) can transform the business, you must first grasp the fundamentals. In this lesson, you'll identify what exactly AI is, as well as gain a high-level understanding of the major concepts that make up AI. This will provide you with a framework for understanding the practical implications of AI, as well as enable you to speak intelligently about this revolutionary phenomenon.

Lesson Objectives
In this lesson, you will:
• Describe the history of AI.
• Describe fundamental AI concepts.


















Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

130 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC A
A Brief History of AI
To begin, you'll focus on what AI is, how it's evolved over the years, and where it's at now. That way, you can place AI in the proper context as you start thinking about ways in which it can help your business.

What Is Artificial Intelligence (AI)?
Artificial intelligence (AI) is, at its core, the ability of machines to exhibit human-like intelligence. What exactly is meant by "human-like intelligence" is a source of much debate-some would even argue that true AI is not yet achievable, given strict requirements for "intelligence." However, there are some factors that make up a common understanding of "intelligence," including:
• The ability to perceive one's own environment.
• The ability to take actions for the purpose of achieving goals.
• The ability to obtain knowledge and apply it to a problem in order to solve that problem.
In a more practical sense, AI is a scientific discipline that governs the study, creation, and operation of machine intelligence. While this discipline has existed for decades, it has seen a recent resurgence in many different facets of society, including business. AI has the potential to revolutionize every sector of industry; in many cases, that revolution has already begun. Although AI is a highly technical field comprising many different complex elements, you don't need a great deal of technical knowledge to understand its impact and how you can leverage it in your organization.

Timeline of AI
It's helpful to contextualize AI as it has progressed over the years. Not only does this reveal where AI has been, it can also reveal where AI is headed.
The history of AI has its roots in antiquity. In a fictional sense, myths like those of Ancient Greece told of artificial beings-a precursor to the modern idea of robots. Realistically, humans have been creating self-operating machines for thousands of years. Later, the rise of formal reasoning and, eventually, the advent of computer science, paved the way for AI. However, it wasn't until after World War II that research into the "thinking machine" really started in earnest.
The following is a timeline of major events, contributors, and technologies, directly leading up to, and in the wake of, the beginning of AI as a scientific discipline.

Time PeriodEvents, Contributors, and Technologies1950Alan Turing proposes the Turing test as a way of measuring the intelligence of a machine.1956The "birth of AI"-the true start of AI as a scientific discipline-occurs at a workshop at Dartmouth College. The term "artificial intelligence" is coined at this workshop.1961Unimate, the first industrial robot, begins work at a General Motors assembly line.1964Joseph Weizenbaum develops ELIZA, one of the first natural language processing programs.1966Development begins on Shakey, the first mobile robot to exhibit rudimentary reasoning abilities.Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 131

Time PeriodEvents, Contributors, and Technologies1973The Lighthill report advises UK Parliament that AI research has failed to achieve its far-reaching objectives.1974-early 1980sDue to the conclusions of the Lighthill report and a general lack of progress in the field of AI, the UK and US governments pull funding from AI research projects. This period is known as the "AI winter."Early 1980s-mid 1980sAI research experiences a boom with the advent of expert systems, which are programmed to answer questions within a specific area of knowledge.Mid 1980s-early 1990sAI research experiences a second winter as personal computing hardware makes specialized expert system hardware a needlessly expensive investment.Mid 1990sThe dramatic increase in computing power and the rise of the Internet begins pulling AI research out of its winter. AI still has little acceptance in the business world, however.1997The Deep Blue supercomputer defeats world champion Garry Kasparov in chess, suggesting a new level of intelligence and creativity in AI systems.2000Cynthia Breazeal publishes a dissertation describing the Kismet robot, a head-shaped machine that simulates human emotions.2004-2005The Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency (DARPA) holds its first Grand Challenge, a competition for self-driving cars. Although no cars complete the course, it helps generate interest in the industry. The second Grand Challenge one year later sees several cars complete the course.2006-2008This period marks the rise of three important technologies that enable AI to flourish in the modern era: cloud computing, big data, and general-purpose graphics processing units (GPUs).2011IBM's Watson supercomputer beats champions Ken Jennings and Brad Rutter at Jeopardy! by a wide margin. Like Deep Blue before it, Watson is a milestone in AI research and application. That same year, Apple(r) releases Siri(r), the first modern virtual assistant. The Google Brain(tm) team, which researches applications of machine learning and deep learning, also forms this year.2016Google's DeepMind(tm) project, which develops the AlphaGo program, defeats champion Lee Sedol at Go. Unlike Deep Blue and Watson, which were designed for specific tasks, DeepMind's programs are more general purpose and use deep learning techniques.2020-presentOpenAI releases GPT-3, a deep learning model that can generate text of such high quality that it is often indistinguishable from text written by a human. It also releases DALL·E and its successor, DALL·E 2, which can generate realistic images based on user text prompts. In 2022, OpenAI releases ChatGPT, a chatbot that can generate human-like responses to queries and requests.
AI-Enabling Technologies: Cloud Computing
As mentioned in the timeline, 2006 to 2008 saw the rise of three of the most important technologies for AI development and research: cloud computing, big data, and general-purpose GPUs. These deserve further explanation.
Cloud computing is an approach to providing computing services and other resources over a network of distributed servers and other infrastructure. Businesses rely on cloud services for many, or even all, of their IT-related needs. Many organizations offload the computing costs of developing AI solutions to the cloud for two reasons: one, because AI, particularly deep learning, requires a lot
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic A

132 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
of resources; and two, because the cloud can efficiently match the ever-changing demand for resources. Also, most organizations don't have the massive infrastructure that cloud providers like Amazon, Google(tm), and Microsoft(r) have.
Think of how you use virtual meeting software like Microsoft(r) Teams(r), Zoom, or something similar. Your organization probably isn't hosting its own virtual meeting servers. Instead, servers in the cloud are establishing and maintaining these virtual meetings on behalf of the participants. So, every time you call someone using these apps, your voice and video are being sent over the Internet to a cloud server, where they are processed by that server and then sent along to the other meeting participants. This is possible thanks to cloud computing.
In 2006, Amazon released Elastic Compute Cloud (EC2(tm)), the flagship service of their Amazon Web Services(tm) (AWS(tm)) platform. This was the first publicly available Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) offering from a cloud vendor. Since then, there have been many more cloud services from different vendors, including services that are specifically designed to support AI development.

AI-Enabling Technologies: GPUs
A graphics processing unit (GPU) is a computer chip designed to process graphics, images, and other visual media. GPUs were originally created to support video game graphics, but most GPUs these days are considered general-purpose GPUs, meaning they can carry out tasks that were traditionally assigned to central processing units (CPUs).
Comparing CPUs and GPUs is a bit like comparing a sports car to a big delivery truck. The sports car (CPU) can carry only a small load, but it is agile and can stop, turn around, and pick up passengers quickly. A big delivery truck (GPU) is not nearly as agile, but can handle a much larger load.
GPUs excel at AI processing tasks whereas CPUs tend to struggle. These tasks require a lot of memory bandwidth, and a lot of linear algebra calculations-both things that GPUs do better than CPUs. Therefore, GPUs have become the de facto processing equipment for AI, particularly deep learning.
If you've played a modern, graphically intensive video game or created/edited images and video on a computer, you have likely used a GPU, even if you weren't aware of it. If not, you've still probably benefited from GPUs indirectly. For example, the recommendations you get from Amazon.com or other online stores are probably using GPUs to process your data and determine the best products to recommend.
Although GPUs have existed since the 1990s, the first general-purpose GPU architecture was released by Nvidia in 2007, named Compute Unified Device Architecture (CUDA). More recently, high-performance GPUs designed to accelerate AI tasks have become much more common.

AI-Enabling Technologies: Big Data
Big data refers to collections of data that are so large and complex that they require advanced tools to process and analyze them. Big data is typically described using several "Vs": volume (the huge amount of data), variety (the many different sources the data can be pulled from), velocity (the high speed at which data processing must occur), and more.
AI is a highly data-driven technology; in fact, data is the first and foremost requirement of creating an AI solution. In general, the more data you have, the more capable your solution will be at solving a given problem. So, before the advent of big data, businesses struggled to collect enough data to build truly useful AI solutions. Now that the volume of available data across all industries has increased dramatically over the past decade, AI has become more viable for even smaller organizations.
Think of all the searches done on Google.com and other search sites. There are billions per day. Now consider that Google does targeted advertising and customized search results based on users' searches. Every time you search for something, you are feeding data into a truly monumental system. This is only possible because of technologies that support the principles of big data.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 133
There is no single event that precipitated the rise of big data, but in 2008, several prominent computer science researchers from the Computing Community Consortium (CCC) published an influential white paper titled "Big-Data Computing: Creating revolutionary breakthroughs in commerce, science, and society." This paper outlined the rising phenomenon of companies collecting and storing volumes of data that were previously unheard of. It also offered predictions and advice for companies as they entered a big data world, including mention of AI applications.


Note: The three enabling technologies just mentioned are often used in combination; for example, a company might use GPUs hosted on a cloud service to process their big data collections.

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 135
TOPIC B
AI Concepts
Now that you know some of the history behind AI, you can start learning its major concepts. Although these concepts are technical in nature, you only need to grasp them from a high level-enough for you to be able to effectively communicate with your colleagues and clients.
General AI and Narrow AI
There are many ways to categorize AI. One way is to define it in terms of its scope and overall ability. When doing so, AI is typically categorized as either being general or narrow.
Artificial general intelligence (AGI), also called general AI or strong AI, refers to machines that have a similar level of cognitive abilities as humans. This means the machine has the ability to use reason-not just to solve one or a few problems, but to extrapolate those reasoning abilities to solve many novel problems. A machine with AGI would be able to fool a human observer into thinking that the machine itself is human. At the moment, AGI is far from being a reality. It is debatable as to whether or not it will ever be a reality. Nevertheless, much philosophical thought and scientific research is being done on the subject, including whether or not a general AI system would truly possess consciousness or sapience.
Narrow AI, also called weak AI, is AI that is able to solve one or a few specific problems that are assigned to it by humans. It can approximate certain human traits, including reason, but it does not truly possess those traits. Instead, a weak AI system is limited in scope and cannot be applied to a general set of tasks like AGI. Still, even systems with limited scope can be very powerful. This is where AI is at now and will likely be for the foreseeable future. So, when people talk about implementing AI in business, they are referring to narrow AI, not the thinking machines of science fiction.
Example
Consider the example of an AI-powered machine cleaning a house. There are autonomous vacuum cleaners on the market today that can navigate a room by themselves, avoiding obstacles and detecting dirty spots on the floor that need cleaning. These AI systems excel at determining how best to go about cleaning a floor. This is an example of narrow AI, because the vacuum robot is good at the one thing it was designed to do. It cannot do anything else, at least not very well.
Now consider a robot that has never vacuumed before. It is able to enter a house (after having been given a key), find the vacuum cleaner in the closet, clean out the dust container if it's full, replace the filter if the vacuum has one and it's old, begin cleaning the floors room by room, empty out the dust that accumulated, put the vacuum back in the closet, then leave the house and lock the door. That robot would possess AGI, because it would be able to perform multiple distinct tasks, similar to how an adult human would vacuum a house if they had never vacuumed it before.

Machine Learning
Machine learning is an AI discipline that makes predictions and other decisions about an environment based on a given set of data. These predictions are made without the aid of instructions programmed directly into the machine itself. Instead, the machine is able to process input data using one or more algorithms-sets of rules for carrying out problem-solving operations
-that can make intelligent decisions about that data and how it pertains to an environment.
The result of the algorithm's calculations on that data is a model. A machine learning model "learns" by measuring how effective (or ineffective) it is at predicting its assigned task, then improves its own predictive abilities based on these performance measurements. As the name
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic B

136 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
implies, machine learning ensures that not only can a machine make intelligent decisions, it can actually get better at making those decisions with time and experience.

Data	Algorithm	Model

Figure 6-1: The general machine learning process.
Machine learning is the predominant form that AI takes in the business world. Most AI projects in an organization will incorporate some type of machine learning, as a machine learning model is the mechanism by which an AI system attempts to solve its assigned problem set.
There are many types of algorithms that implement machine learning, and each one might be more appropriate than others in certain situations. This course won't go into the technical details, but there are a few more aspects of machine learning worth diving into.


Note: Machine learning is occasionally abbreviated as ML.
Machine Learning Datasets
As mentioned, machine learning models are created by putting input data through an algorithm. But what exactly is being "learned" by the machine? Consider the following example: You have a spreadsheet of health data for individuals who participated in a study. The data is in a table, with each person as a row and various characteristics of each person as columns (e.g., weight, height, blood pressure level, etc.). There are two dimensions to this data:
• Example-the individual instance of data. This corresponds to rows in a table, and in the health records scenario, refers to individual people.
• Feature-each measurable property or characteristic of an example. This corresponds to columns in a table, and in the health records scenario, refers to a person's body weight, height, blood pressure level, etc.
Put together, this dataset is fed into the algorithm as input. The resulting model identifies the patterns in the features for each example, and uses these patterns to determine how to make predictions on new data. For example, the model might learn how a person's body weight relates to their blood pressure level-not just for a specific person, but in general. That way, the model can make informed predictions about a new patient based on their body weight and blood pressure level.
The process by which a model learns from input data is called training.






Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Deep Learning
Deep learning is a type of machine learning that makes decisions using multiple layers of information. These layers are arranged as a hierarchy of decisions or other tasks, where the conclusions drawn from a lower layer can influence the conclusions drawn at higher layers. This makes a deep learning model more complex than a traditional machine learning model.
Consider the example of a model that processes an image of a human face. It will organize aspects of a face into different layers, starting with simple shapes like lines and curves and moving on from there. Eventually, at the highest layers, it will start identifying more recognizable features: eyes, lips, ears, a nose, etc. The deep learning model eventually is able to determine whether or not an image contains a human face based on what it has learned.
Deep learning moves past tabular datasets like the health records shown earlier. Instead, deep learning datasets usually consist of unstructured data-images, video, audio, text, and other data that can't fit neatly into a spreadsheet.




















Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic B

138 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	

Figure 6-3: A deep learning model builds its understanding of an image through layers.

Note: The "deep" in deep learning refers to the many layers involved in the learning process.
Deep Learning Applications
There are many ways to apply deep learning to solving problems, but most applications of deep learning center on the following disciplines:
• Natural language processing (NLP): The discipline concerned with how computers analyze and apply human languages. There are actually many sub-disciplines that make up the field of NLP, including text analysis, speech recognition, natural language understanding, natural language generation, and natural language translation.
• Computer vision: The discipline that involves computers understanding images and video at a high level. Like NLP, computer vision is a discipline that drives many more fields and technologies, including object recognition, object classification, image generation, motion detection, trajectory estimation, video tracking, and navigation.
• Robotics: The discipline that involves studying, designing, and operating robots. There is no universally agreed-upon definition of "robot," however. In general, it can be said that a robot is a machine that can act in the physical world with some degree of autonomy (i.e., independent of human control).


Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 139
Generative AI
One particularly popular application of deep learning in recent years is generative AI. Generative AI refers to deep learning models that are designed to generate any kind of content-text, programming code, audio, images, video, and so on. GPT-3, DALL·E, and ChatGPT are examples of generative AI because they can create lifelike examples of content.
Most generative models are trained on massive amounts of data that correspond to whatever the model is designed to generate. A model like GPT-3 that generates text is trained on textual sources like scientific papers, textbooks, novels, social media posts, chat logs, and potentially much more. A model like DALL·E that generates images is trained on photographs, hand drawings, computer-generated images (CGI), and more. Either one could use NLP, computer vision, or a mix of the two.
The power of generative AI is that any user, regardless of technical ability, can "ask" the model to create whatever it is the user wants. This is typically done by providing the model an input prompt, usually a short textual description of the thing that you'd like to generate. The model translates the words and phrases you've provided in the prompt into some type of content that (ideally) embodies those words and phrases. You might prompt the model to write a science fiction short story about explorers in space who discover an alien civilization, or you might prompt a different model to create a realistic photograph of a monkey with a small hat typing at a typewriter in an office. You're only limited by how effective the model is at understanding your prompt and translating it into useful content.
At first it might seem like generative AI is more of a toy or novelty, but it actually holds a great deal of potential in business applications. Being able to quickly generate images, such as logos or stock images, can greatly reduce the time and money that it would require for a human to create them.
The same is true for text, as being able to quickly generate marketing copy, press releases, or responses to customers is also valuable to many organizations.

Prompt: a photograph of a monkey typing at a typewriter, wearing a small hat, in an office, realistic, highly detailed

Figure 6-4: The following images were generated using an open-source model similar to DALL·E called Stable Diffusion. The prompt used to generate the images is also shown.

Note: Generative models don't provide perfect results every time, and there is a sort of art to writing effective prompts.Large Language Models (LLM)
A related concept to generative AI is the large language model (LLM). LLMs are specifically designed to analyze patterns in very large bodies of text in order to determine how best to predict some kind of textual output. LLMs like GPT-3 and ChatGPT are able to determine what word should come next in a sentence based on various factors, including the user's input prompt and the context of the other words that have already been generated.

Machine Learning vs. Deep Learning
The most notable difference between traditional machine learning and deep learning has to do with features. Remember, in traditional machine learning, a model is given a set of features and learns their patterns. In deep learning, you don't explicitly give the model a defined set of features. Instead, the deep learning model itself learns the features inherent in the data. It is able to extract the features from the raw data it is given without human guidance.
Recall the example of a deep learning model determining if an image contains a human face. How would you describe someone's face in an image? You could focus on one aspect at a time, like eyes. You might measure each component of the eye-iris, retina, sclera, etc. You might also record the eye's color. But what if someone is facing a certain direction in one image but a different direction in another? What if the person's eyes are closed? What if the lighting is changing the color of the eye? There are so many variables involved in an image that it's just not feasible for you to do all this yourself.

Traditional Machine Learning

Input image	Human identifies features	Model learns patterns	Model makes decision

Deep Learning



Input image	Model extracts features and
learns patterns

Model makes decision


Figure 6-5: Traditional machine learning vs. deep learning. Deep learning is much more appropriate in this scenario, as manually identifying features in an image is usually not feasible.
This is where deep learning shines. The deep learning model has to decide for itself what constitutes a "feature" of a human face. Without a human operator telling it how, the deep learning model has learned what a human face looks like.

The Choice Between Machine Learning and Deep Learning
The choice between traditional machine learning and deep learning is highly dependent on the data you're working with and what you want to do with that data.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 141
Traditional machine learning is more commonly applied to smaller datasets in which an expert has already determined the features relevant to solving the problem. For example, customer survey responses, patient health information, and bank account activity are some common applications for traditional machine learning because you'd typically know what characteristics are useful for predicting customer preferences, health issues, and fraud.
On the other hand, deep learning is a better choice in situations where it's difficult to determine the characteristics that contribute to a prediction. For example, object recognition in images and video is a common application of deep learning because you don't necessarily know how to precisely describe a complex object. Likewise, deep learning is also used to help machines understand and generate human speech, as language is also a very complex concept. In addition, deep learning is more desirable when you have massive amounts of data, time, and resources at your disposal.

How AI Concepts Relate
You might still be wondering how these three concepts relate to one another. You can think of them as a hierarchy:
• Artificial intelligence is the umbrella term that encompasses many different techniques.
• Machine learning is one subset of AI, though a fairly prominent one in today's world.
• Deep learning is a type of machine learning that can solve more complex problems.

Figure 6-6: How AI, machine learning, and deep learning relate.


Note: In some cases, people use the term "machine learning" to mean the overall discipline, whereas other times they may use "machine learning" to mean traditional machine learning (i.e., not deep learning). Don't be afraid to ask for clarification when you're speaking with someone.

Machine Learning Approaches
Regardless of whether you're using traditional machine learning or deep learning, you will need to take one of three approaches to building a model:
• Supervised learning
• Unsupervised learning
• Reinforcement learning
It's necessary to understand these approaches from a high level, as they directly affect the kind of problems your AI systems can solve.

Supervised Machine Learning
Supervised learning is an approach to machine learning that involves providing known values of the variable you are trying to predict as part of the input data. Recall the health records example: each data example had several features like weight, height, and blood pressure. You want your model to predict whether or not a new patient has heart disease given these features. In the data you use as training, you have a final column that records a "yes" or "no" as to whether or not this person has heart disease. This is called the label, or the ground truth, and it is a requirement of supervised learning.
By giving the model labels for each data example, it is able to identify the patterns not just among the features, but the among the features as they relate to the very thing the model is meant to predict. The model is able to learn how well (or how poorly) it is able to predict the label, gradually improving as a result.
There are two primary applications of supervised learning you should be aware of:
• Regression, in which a supervised model is attempting to predict some numerical value. Examples include predicting tomorrow's temperature, predicting how many customers will patronize a restaurant on a given night, predicting how much product inventory will be available in the coming months, and so on.
• Classification, in which a supervised model is attempting to place a data example in a class or category. Examples include classifying a patient as having heart disease or not, determining what type of clothing appears in a product image, determining whether or not a job applicant is worth considering for the interview process, and so on.




Labeled dataset






Model learns patterns from labeled data

Model outputs classification


WeightHeight234.563New, unlabeled data sent to model

Figure 6-7: Training a supervised learning model to classify a patient as having heart disease or not having heart disease.

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 143
Unsupervised Machine Learning
Unsupervised learning is a machine learning approach in which the model is not given label values. In other words, the model isn't explicitly told what it needs to predict, as this information is not provided as part of the training data. The model is still given the examples and a set of features, but no label. There are many reasons why the label might be absent-maybe it wasn't collected with the rest of the data, or maybe labeling the data is infeasible. In any case, a dataset without ground truth cannot be applied to regression or classification problems.
Without a set of labels to learn from, an unsupervised model must instead learn from the patterns in the features. The main application of unsupervised learning is called clustering, and it identifies commonalities by grouping like data together into clusters. The model can also consider how these commonalities exist in new sets of data in order to group them into their relevant clusters.
Unsupervised learning is useful in areas such as customer segmentation. There isn't necessarily a specific thing that the business wants to predict; they just want to place customers into distinct groups so they can tailor their customer service and marketing approach to customers based on their attributes and behaviors.

Figure 6-8: Training an unsupervised learning model to cluster customers.
Reinforcement Learning
Reinforcement learning, the third of the main machine learning approaches, is the process by which a software agent acts in an environment in order to obtain a reward. This involves the following steps:
1. The agent selects an action from an available set of actions.
2. The agent takes the action in the environment.
3. The environment changes state in some way.
4. The agent is rewarded or not, depending on how its action affected the state of the environment.
5. The process repeats.







Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals | Topic B

144 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	





Figure 6-9: A machine being trained to play chess using reinforcement learning.
Ultimately, the goal of the agent is to maximize the amount of rewards it accumulates. So, it will learn which actions generate the most rewards, and thereby become better at interacting with the given environment. A reward is basically a function that indicates to the agent how it should act-i.e., what actions are desirable and worth repeating in the future.
Reinforcement learning is optimal in situations where the machine can learn effectively through trial and error. Robots that move or perform other independent actions in the physical world often use reinforcement learning, as do computers that play games like chess, checkers, and Go. While less popular than other forms of machine learning in the business world, reinforcement learning is still used in areas like personalizing customer experiences, planning optimal travel routes, and optimizing financial investment and trading decisions.











Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

That way, you can identify which products are the most popular among your highly engaged customers.

Is this an example of supervised, unsupervised, or reinforcement learning? Explain why.

Lesson 6: AI Fundamentals |

Functions of AI in Business
Lesson Time: 1 hour, 30 minutes




Lesson Introduction
Artificial intelligence (AI) has many applications across a wide variety of industries and sectors. In this lesson, you'll explore several of the most common ways in which AI enables new business functions or enhances existing functions. This will get you thinking about how AI can fulfill your own business needs and goals.

Lesson Objectives
In this lesson, you will:
• Identify how AI can be used to improve the user experience.
• Identify how AI can help segment audiences.
• Identify how AI can help secure organizational assets.
• Identify how AI can help optimize business processes.















Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

148 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC A
Improve User Experiences
The first set of business functions you'll look at are those that improve the user experience by leveraging AI. These types of improvement are great at increasing customer engagement and promoting brand loyalty.

Natural Language Processing
Recall that one of the applications of deep learning is natural language processing (NLP), a discipline concerned with how computers analyze and apply human languages. Some of the sub-disciplines that make up the field of NLP include:
• Text analysis
• Speech recognition
• Natural language understanding
• Natural language generation
• Natural language translation
If you've ever used a text-to-speech or speech-to-text program, you've leveraged technology that is made possible by one or more of the previous NLP techniques. Computers can attempt to recognize language whether it is written or spoken, then analyze that language for familiar linguistic traits like syntax, semantics, phonetics, grammar, spelling, and more. In some applications, computers can use this analysis to generate new speech or written text that approximates how humans actually talk or write.
NLP seeks to bridge the gap between how computers communicate and how humans communicate. It can enable people to more easily understand machines, and vice versa. NLP also helps people with disabilities communicate with other people through the use of a computer.

Chatbots
A chatbot is an NLP system that can simulate real-time communication with users over a text-based chat. In the past, rudimentary automated chat systems would just give canned responses to certain questions; on the other hand, AI-driven chatbots actually exhibit a degree of intelligence in how they interpret and respond to user messages. Even though the same basic idea can be communicated in many different ways, a chatbot will be able to "understand" the core idea behind a user's message and then decide which response is most suitable.
There are many reasons why you might implement one or more chatbots in your organization. Some major examples include:
• Customer service: Chatbots can alleviate the burden of your customer service team by being the first line of contact between the customer and the organization. The user might be directed to a contact form on your website or app, which then runs the chatbot to interact with the user. The chatbot then interprets the customer's question or concern and provides the appropriate response, such as a troubleshooting tip or who to contact for more information. In many cases, businesses use customer service chatbots to filter out customers who have common issues; only the most unique or difficult questions are escalated to a human representative.
• Appointment scheduling: Businesses that require their customers to book appointments, such as in the healthcare industry, can implement chatbots to streamline the process. This might be a more attractive option than having the customer select an available time from a list or calendar, as the chatbot can be more helpful at offering alternative times or responding to queries.
• Human resources management: There are some tasks that employees regularly engage HR with that can be automated through the use of a chatbot. For example, employees could schedule
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 149
vacation time or sick time with the chatbot. Again, this might be preferable to filling out a form, since the chatbot can be trained to identify issues with the request, such as someone trying to take an entire month off in a busy part of the year.
• Information research: Some industries require quick access to accurate information, such as an automotive engineer researching information about parts that will be used in next year's model. An organization can build a chatbot to simulate expertise in one or more fields by taking a user's query and determining how best to extract the desired information from available data stores. This can greatly reduce the time an employee spends looking up information that is hard to find.
ChatGPT
In November 2022, OpenAI released ChatGPT. ChatGPT is a generative large language model (LLM) based on GPT-3 (specifically, version 3.5) that represents a major leap forward in chatbot systems. It is able to take user input and produce responses that are more realistic and useful than any chatbot before it. Part of the reason why is because ChatGPT is able to "remember" earlier parts of a conversation, enabling it to maintain important context, whereas most other chatbots consider each user message independently when forming a response.
ChatGPT has many use cases in business beyond the chatbot use cases previously mentioned. It can also be used to write essays, articles, technical documentation, and even working code. However, ChatGPT is not without its flaws. If the user asks a factual question, the model may return an answer that sounds very plausible, but is incorrect. Still, it has been shown to exhibit an impressive ability to mimic human communication. OpenAI plans to continue work on the model and improve it in the years to come.


Note: For more information on ChatGPT and to try it out, see https://openai.com/blog/ chatgpt/.
Agent Assistance
Agent assistance is automation technology that helps human agents in a call center do their jobs more efficiently and effectively. This can take many forms, including:
• Call context: Calls in a call center are often transferred between agents or departments, and customers can become frustrated when the next person they talk to doesn't have all of the relevant information. An agent assistance system can provide the proper context of a call to any agent the call gets transferred to, including who the customer is, what their general issue is, and any other pertinent information-all of which changes dynamically as the customer is routed from agent to agent.
• Immediate responses: Just like a chatbot can address simple or common customer service requests, an agent assistance system can automatically send the proper response to a customer-whether it's greeting them, saying goodbye, apologizing for any inconvenience, prompting them for more information, and so on. Not only does this make the human agent's job easier, it means the call goes quicker and is less likely to waste the customer's time.
• Suggested responses: Instead of just replying automatically using a pre-recorded message, the AI system can suggest to the human agent how they should respond to the customer. This suggestion can take the customer's query into account while also looking up useful information in a centralized repository. That way, the human agent doesn't waste time digging through a database for the proper information.
• Live transcription and summarization: Using various speech recognition techniques, the system can transcribe the call to make later reference easier for both the system and a human agent. Alternatively, the system can create a summary of the call, including the key points of what was discussed and whether or not the issue was resolved. That way, the agent can quickly reference a past call if the same customer calls again.


Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic A

150 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Sentiment Analysis
Sentiment analysis is the technique of determining the attitudes and feelings someone has toward something. In the world of business, this usually means assessing how a customer feels about the business or its products and services. This can be everything from a simple positive or negative sentiment-the customer does or does not like their experience-to more complex classifications like displeasure, disgust, ambivalence, satisfaction, happiness, and so on.
Most sentiment analysis incorporates NLP, as most consumers' opinions are expressed through direct communication-usually through social media or a review on a website. The NLP system can pick up on certain key words, as well as the context in which those words appear, to determine how the person feels. For example, a review that begins with the sentence, "I usually love this company's products, but . . ." would probably be classified as a negative sentiment if the AI is properly trained.
There are various reasons to apply sentiment analysis in an organization, including:
• Customer retention: Obviously, unhappy customers are less likely to continue to do business with you. You can attempt to address their misgivings in various ways, like offering them coupons for future purchases. However, customers don't always express these sentiments in ways that are easy for a human to assess, which is why NLP systems with sentiment analysis capabilities are an effective alternative.
• Employee retention: Just as customers express their satisfaction or dissatisfaction with a product, so too do employees express these sentiments about their jobs. You might use an AI system to assess the tone of a meeting transcript (or the live meeting itself) to determine how your workforce is generally feeling. You might then choose to assign resources for morale-boosting exercises if morale is low.
• Product development strategy: The public can influence the strategic direction your company takes with regard to product development. For example, if people are posting positive opinions on social media about certain types of products you sell, you may choose to focus on developing more versions of those products while reducing development effort on products that have not been as warmly received. Assessing the sentiment of thousands or even millions of social media posts is a job for an AI, not a human.
• Recommendations: Sentiment analysis is also used to recommend products and services to people. If a user posts a positive review of a movie on a streaming service, an AI system may be able to recommend similar movies to them. And vice versa, the system would take similar movies out of the recommended pool if the user posted a negative review.

Emotion AI
Emotion AI is very similar to sentiment analysis. Some experts see them as different terms referring to the same thing, and some see one as a subset of the other. However, many AI researchers are starting to draw a distinction: Sentiment analysis focuses on analyzing language through NLP, whereas emotion AI is mostly concerned with analyzing facial expressions and other facial features. Both, however, have the same purpose: to determine how someone feels about something.
When analyzing facial features, emotion AI applies computer vision techniques like object recognition and object classification. For example, it can detect the furrowing of a person's brow to interpret this to mean anger or some other show of displeasure. Emotion AI can also incorporate motion detection as part of its analysis. For example, it can detect the difference between a smile and a laugh by looking at the movement of the person's jaw rather than just focus on the shape of the mouth.
The applications of emotion AI in business are similar to those of standard, NLP-based sentiment analysis. However, it has proven more difficult to implement, not to mention controversial. After all, many people are uncomfortable with the idea that a business is taking a picture or video of their face and using it for marketing purposes. So, you may have less opportunity to use emotion AI for things like customer retention, recommendations, and so on.

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 151


Note: Emotion AI can also incorporate the analysis of more subtle physical cues, like the tone of someone's voice.
Recommendation Systems
A recommendation system, or recommender system, suggests items, services, and other things of interest to users. Recommendation systems powered by machine learning are able to more accurately determine what most interests the targeted user(s), or what recommendations users will respond most positively to. Ideally, this makes the system more useful to both the user (who is more likely to see something they like) and the business (who is more likely to generate sales or interest in their brand).
Recommendation systems typically work on the principle that, faced with an overwhelming selection to choose from, most users would rather have those selections filtered to show only the few that are most relevant to them. This can be everything from what product the user might want to buy next from a store, to what movie a user might want to watch next on a streaming platform, and much more.
As mentioned before, recommendation systems can rely on sentiment analysis, but they can also rely on other behavioral attributes. For example, if a user watches a movie on a streaming service for only a few minutes and then quits to watch something else, it could indicate to a recommender model what the user is or is not interested in seeing next.
Some examples of recommendation systems in business include:
• Content recommendation: The aforementioned example of a streaming service suggesting a movie to watch is a classic example of a recommendation engine. The business isn't necessarily generating more direct sales, but it is increasing engagement, which can lead to better customer retention.
• Product recommendation: Other than recommending content within an existing subscription, recommendation systems can be used to suggest any type of product or service to a customer whose attributes are known to the model-their demographics, how they interact with the business, etc. This usually leads to increased sales, as customers are more likely to buy the product or service relevant to them.
• Social networking: Recommendation engines can "match" people who have common interests or goals. In the business world, this can mean bringing people with relevant skills and talents together to work on a project or brainstorm ideas. Recommending the right people can lead to more effective results and reduce a lot of time spent searching for qualified personnel.
• Research: Academics, scientists, and other people in technical or information-oriented fields may spend a lot of time on research. Recommendation systems can find high-quality results that pertain to the person's area of research much faster than any human. The results themselves may also be of better quality than what a human could find.

Digital Personalization
Recommendation systems are just one way that businesses use AI to personalize the user experience. There are many other facets to digital personalization, including serving specific content (e.g., search results that are of specific interest to the user), hiding specific content (e.g., search results that are not of interest to the user), rearranging content on an app, changing the visual theme of an app, and so on. All of these changes can be made at a group level or even down to individual users.
AI systems determine how to personalize user experiences based on a number of features that build a user's profile. Aspects of that profile may include:
• The user's geolocation.
• The user's race, gender, and age demographics.
• The user's socioeconomic status.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

• The user's browsing or online shopping history.
• The user's general sentiment toward the business.
The system can tailor the user's experience in such a way that maximizes engagement with the app through metrics like click-through rate (CTR) or watch time. The model improves as it learns what experience is best suited to what users.

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic A

154 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC B
Segment Audiences
Another business function enhanced by AI is audience segmentation, also known as customer segmentation or market segmentation. This can lead to better customer retention and higher sales, among other benefits.

Customer Segmentation
Businesses focus their marketing efforts on specific segments of their customer base. Traditionally, these segments have been created using overt customer attributes, particularly demographics like age and gender. The two downsides of this approach are: it ignores more subtle factors that might be useful in grouping customers; and it can only handle a small number of factors, reducing the complexity of the groups.
Both of these concerns are addressed by AI-powered systems that do customer segmentation. They are able to build more intricate and useful groups by considering hundreds, perhaps thousands of factors that go into what makes a "customer"-not just overt factors like age, but also factors like lifestyle, general worldview, sentiment toward the business, and so on.
There are several ways AI can intelligently segment customers, but perhaps the most common tactic is to cluster customers according to a set of their features. In this unsupervised approach, you can train a model to place customers into one of several groups, where customers in a group have similar values for all relevant features.
One interesting aspect of clustering is that you can engineer the clusters in different ways. You can set a hard number of clusters from the beginning-for example, if you only want to work with a total of nine groups, you can tell the model to assign a customer to one of nine clusters.
Alternatively, machine learning practitioners can analyze a model using various metrics to determine the "optimal" number of clusters. You may go this route if you don't have a specific number of segments in mind, and want to make sure each segment is as distinct as possible.


















Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 155

Figure 7-1: Customers segmented using a clustering model. Note that the names of each cluster are based on an analysis of the customers in each cluster.

Predictive Segmentation
Unsupervised clustering is not the only way AI can improve customer segmentation. Depending on your available dataset(s) and what you're trying to accomplish, you might also be able to use supervised techniques like classification and regression to generate customer segments based on some label that the machine learning model predicts.
For example, you have a dataset of many customer attributes, and one of those attributes you consider to be the label you're trying to predict. Perhaps it's "Did the customer return to make more purchases, yes or no?" You want to be able to predict whether future customers will return to purchase more items. So, this is a classification problem. But, you can also parlay the results into an opportunity for customer segmentation. Customers the model predicted will return could go in one segment, whereas customers the model predicted will not return could go in another. Or, you might incorporate the predictions into a clustering model to combine both unsupervised and supervised learning. In either case, the predictive system has improved your ability to segment customers.

Targeted Campaigns and Customer Profiles
Targeted campaigns are built from customer segments. One segment receives one campaign, whereas another segment receives a different campaign. But AI can be involved in more than just the direct segmentation aspect-it can also be useful in shaping the content of the campaigns according to the characteristics of each segment they target.
Consider a scenario where you use unsupervised learning to cluster your customer base. Each customer is put into, for example, one of nine clusters. But, clustering by itself does not label each cluster in a meaningful way. It simply does the grouping. So, how will you shape your marketing campaigns to accommodate each cluster?
Cluster analysis can help you learn more about the common attributes of the customers in each cluster. What age group do the customers fall into? What is the most common gender in the cluster? What general location are the customers from? A practitioner can perform statistical analyses on these factors and any others that are included in the input data to help generate a customer profile
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic B

156 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
or persona. The profile/persona describes each segment using words rather than just raw numbers. It is not a depiction of a real person, but a depiction of a fictitious person who embodies the traits common to people in that segment. Using profiles and personas, you can start to get a better idea of what language and content might be most appropriate for a certain campaign.

Figure 7-2: A customer profile built from traits common to a specific market segment.
