Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic B

158 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC C
Secure Assets
Another major application of AI in business centers on security. Intelligent security systems can protect key assets against attack and exploitation, preventing the business from losing money, time, and its foothold in the market.

Risk Management
Risk management is the cyclical process of identifying, assessing, analyzing, and responding to risks. It is one of the most fundamental processes in an organization's cybersecurity operations, as risk permeates all aspects of business.
Examples of AI applied to risk management include:
• Risk detection: The first step in addressing risk is to actually identify what is a risk and where it's coming from. An AI system can assess various dimensions of the organization's operations to identify risks that have a historical basis. It can also continuously monitor the organization for new risks as they appear.
• Risk analysis: Revealing the attributes of a risk can be difficult because they are often complex and subtle. However, AI systems that have been trained on historical risk attributes may be able to categorize or cluster certain behaviors or entities in ways that make it easier to understand the risk. For example, an AI-powered analysis might be able to show how features like system vulnerabilities, equipment failure rates, network instability, etc., can lead to risk.
• Risk scoring: There are several methods for scoring risk, or evaluating its likelihood and impact in one calculation. AI systems can accurately assess the factors that contribute the most to likelihood and impact, such as the information revealed through a risk analysis. The output of the model might be a literal numeric score, or it might be more qualitative in nature (e.g., low risk vs. medium risk vs. high risk).
• Risk response: Knowing about risk is one thing, but knowing how to respond is just as important. The organization can avoid, transfer, mitigate, or accept a risk. Choosing which approach is not always obvious, but a classification model may be able to suggest the optimal approach with more confidence than a human analyst.

Threat Analysis
Threat analysis, also called threat modeling, is the study of people, events, and technologies that can harm an organization's assets-its data, networks, systems, employees, etc. Modern cybersecurity threats are growing more and more complex; many organizations dedicate time and resources to uncovering information about those threats so that the organization can better prepare its defenses.
Threat analysis has been a staple of organizational cybersecurity for many years, but only recently have security experts begun to leverage AI in this area. AI-based threat analysis systems can not only enhance existing capabilities, they can also deploy novel approaches to detecting and assessing threats.
Some example applications include:
• Threat classification: Using a supervised classification model, AI can help to categorize threats in many different ways depending on the data used as input. Common features that describe a threat might be its vector (i.e., the path it takes during an attack), its level of severity, its direct and indirect target(s), the projected damage it will inflict (e.g., in lost revenue), its point of origin (whether physical or virtual), and many more. The simplest classification result would be to mark the entity or event as either "is a threat" or "is not a threat," but more sophisticated systems can classify threats in more qualitative ways. For example, the system might label an event as a web-
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 159
based attack, a password cracking attack, a data exfiltration attack, a backdoor attack, and so on. This can help you prioritize certain threats over others, or provide guidance on how to deal with specific types of threats.
• Threat ranking: An alternative approach would be to rank a threat instead of classifying it using qualitative terms. A threat can be ranked by its severity or its likelihood of success, and the cybersecurity team can triage threats according to these rankings. For example, a labeled dataset might assign a severity "score" to each threat, which a regression model can use to calculate the scores for new threats.
• Threat segmentation: Obtaining or assigning a meaningful label to cybersecurity threats can be difficult, which is why unsupervised clustering approaches are also commonly used to place threats in groups. An analyst can extract insights from the nature of each threat according to how it was assigned to a cluster, which can in turn prompt the cybersecurity team to take certain preventative or responsive actions.
• Threat research: Like other forms of data extraction and research, a machine learning model can greatly assist human cybersecurity personnel in retrieving relevant information about threats much more quickly and effectively than they would be able to do on their own. This keeps the personnel focused on proactively addressing threats rather than wasting time combing through voluminous threat databases.

IT Asset Management (ITAM)
IT asset management (ITAM) is the process of identifying, inventorying, tracking, and maintaining information technology assets as they are used across the organization. The purpose is to ensure that these assets are being used effectively across their entire lifecycle and to reduce the incidence of lost, damaged, or inoperable equipment.
Depending on the size of the organization and its available IT personnel, traditional ITAM processes may be inefficient, tedious, or even non-existent. AI can enhance ITAM in several ways, including:
• User requests: Users put in requests for new and upgraded hardware and software, and it can be difficult to prioritize (or deny) certain requests as compared to others. An AI system can consider numerous features, such as the user's job role, their daily duties, their history with IT assets, the number of available assets relevant to the request, etc., to either rank requests or classify them in different ways. That way, IT personnel aren't spending a lot of time deciding who should get what and when.
• Fault prediction: Some AI systems can continuously or periodically scan assigned devices to perform a "health check" of sorts. For example, the system might keep track of a laptop's lifetime service, the number of reads/writes to the storage drive, the presence of malicious software, the score it receives on certain computing benchmarks, and so on, to predict the likelihood that the laptop will need to be replaced soon. That way, the IT team doesn't have to wait until the user informs them that they need a replacement, avoiding a situation where the user is stuck without a functioning device waiting for a replacement to arrive.
• Asset procurement: When ordering a new asset or a replacement, IT personnel can struggle to choose the right product or the right vendor to purchase it from. AI systems could be trained to identify the most suitable way to procure an asset based on the recipient's features, the features of any asset it is replacing, or features that describe the daily tasks that the asset will need to perform.
• Compliance issue identification: Most organizations have acceptable usage policies that they expect employees to follow while using assets like equipment and software. An AI system could be trained to spot behavior that violates these rules and either block the behavior or notify a member of the IT team. If the source of compliance is a law or regulation instead of just organizational policy, having an AI-powered auditing process becomes even more attractive.



Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic C

160 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Fraud Identification
Fraud is a major problem in certain industries and sectors, particularly banking and other financial institutions. Poring through thousands or millions of transactions to look for instances of fraud is just not feasible for most organizations, so AI systems are put in place to streamline the process.
Some examples of fraud identification using AI include:
• Transaction assessment: Banks use fraud detection systems to identify transactions that are likely to indicate malicious actions taken against users or the bank itself. The AI system can draw from historical records of accounts or transactions that are known to engage in fraud, which it uses to classify activity as being legitimate or fraudulent. Or, it can rank activity on a sliding scale, where the activity has some likelihood of being fraudulent.
• Outlier detection: In the absence of labeled fraud data, AI-powered fraud detection systems can also learn to divide accounts or transactions into groups that can indicate suspicious activity. For example, most transactions might be placed into one of several well-represented groups, whereas a few outliers are in one or more small groups. These outliers might suggest fraudulent behavior since they deviate from the norm so significantly.
• Signature analysis: AI systems can be trained with computer vision capabilities to identify fraudulent signatures on official documents like checks, contracts, tax forms, and ballots. A deep learning model can pick up on visual indicators of a forged signature that are too subtle for human eyes to detect.
• Identity theft detection: Some AI systems build profiles of typical behavior for their users, and any pattern of behavior that deviates from this profile can indicate identity theft. For example, a bank might cluster users according to their purchase history. If the model abruptly reassigns the user to a different cluster due to some change in their pattern of behavior, it could indicate that a malicious user has hijacked their account.

Security Notifications and Alerts
Security professionals and other IT personnel need to stay well informed about potential issues in their IT environments, particularly when it comes to behavior that could indicate a breach.
However, the challenge comes in finding the balance between too few alerts (leading to missed problems) and too many alerts (leading to time wasted on false alarms). Thankfully, AI can provide support.
Some ways in which AI can improve security notifications and alerts are as follows:
• Alert sorting and filtering: AI system can sort and filter alerts to minimize false positives and maximize true positives. The nature of the alert and how such alerts are usually addressed by the security team might go into its decision to suppress or emphasize an alert.
• Security dashboards: Getting random alerts out of context will slow down personnel's response to an event and potentially cost the organization precious time in an emergency. However, an AI system that can quickly and effectively summarize an issue in a dashboard will be helpful to personnel who review the alerts. The system can determine which components and metrics are most helpful in displaying on the dashboard, and how (e.g., through a visual graph or as raw numbers/words).
• Notification dissemination: Sending an alert to every member of the security team, or to all of the devices and accounts the recipients use, is not a good use of an alert system. It can waste people's time and lead to communication issues. An AI system can be trained to categorize an alert based on several features, including its severity, which systems it affects, and so on, to determine which individuals are the most important to contact, and where they are most likely to need to see the alert (e.g., on their phone vs. on their laptop).
• Remediation suggestions: Security personnel might be satisfied with being informed about a potential issue, but an AI system can go one step further and suggest how to remediate the issue. Based on the cause of the alert or the behavior it describes, there may be one or a few common solutions. The security team can take these into consideration when they receive the alert, enabling them to act more quickly.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 161
Cloud Security and Virtualization
Cloud security is another business function that can be enhanced by AI. It includes everything
from controlling access to a cloud service's management interface, to hardening the virtual servers that run in the cloud, to protecting the network that underlies an organization's cloud operations, to preventing unauthorized access or modification to data stored by and transmitted through the cloud, and much more.
In the application of cloud security, AI can improve event sorting and filtering, anomalous activity detection, application programming interface (API) protection, and data and privacy protection.
Aside from security, AI can enhance cloud virtualization as well. Virtualization is the process of creating an abstract representation of a computing environment, and it is the fundamental technology underlying the cloud. AI can work with cloud virtualization to enhance provisioning and deprovisioning, resource allocation, and server and application management.

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic C

TOPIC D

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 163

Optimize Processes
AI is also sought after for its ability to optimize many different types of business processes through automation. This can greatly reduce costs and open up new and profitable business opportunities that wouldn't otherwise be available.

Industrial Robots
Industrial robots and other warehouse machines are a common application of machine learning, particularly reinforcement learning. The machine can perform a task in a physical environment while learning which tasks are most desirable. Many of these robots use computer vision to "see" the world around them so that they can move without hitting objects or people.
Some example applications of industrial robots include:
• Welding: Robots can weld components together without a human remotely controlling the machine. Because welding involves very high heat, it can be dangerous to a human worker. The robot can therefore minimize safety issues. Robots are also more efficient at welding than any human, which can increase productivity.
• Assembly and disassembly: In manufacturing plants, humans are traditionally needed to assemble parts on an assembly line. However, industrial robots with robotic "arms" can articulate those arms faster than a human, making them much more productive at assembling and disassembling parts. The robot scans the parts as they come down the line, then determines how to assemble or disassemble them based on their reinforcement learning reward system.
• Inspection and testing: Regardless of whether a product is made on an assembly line, it is a common practice to inspect products-either all of them or a random selection-for defects. Sometimes, those defects are obvious, but in other cases, they can be very difficult for a human to perceive. Inspection robots may be able to scan a product in much more detail and identify subtle cracks or deformities. They are also effective at testing products in extreme ways, such as blasting them with high heat to see how they withstand a fire.
• Packaging and labeling: Once a product is ready to be packaged (perhaps after having been assembled from smaller parts and then tested), a robot on an assembly line can then package and label the product for shipping out to a vendor. Again, a properly trained robot can do this much more quickly than a human and is less likely to make mistakes.

Autonomous Vehicles
An autonomous vehicle is any vehicle that can drive without human input. They are also called self-driving vehicles. Using techniques like reinforcement learning and computer vision, an autonomous vehicle is able to sense the road or ground beneath it, as well as the surrounding environment, and react accordingly. It can speed up and slow down as necessary to obey speed limits, take turns, avoid other cars, or stop short of obstacles. It can also adapt to weather conditions like slick roads caused by recent rainfall. Autonomous vehicles are also designed with safety in mind, particularly when it comes to minimizing the harm of pedestrians and passengers.
Some common applications of autonomous vehicles in business include:
• Product transport and freight: Human truckers may be overworked and more prone to making mistakes, whereas an automated truck does not have these problems. It can operate without rest and therefore can minimize accidents while also saving time.
• Delivery: On a smaller scale (as far as distance traveled), many businesses rely on cars to deliver products locally to their customers. Think of food or grocery delivery services. Automating the

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic D

164 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
trip from business to home and vice versa can greatly increase the efficiency of travel, leading to more satisfied customers and a more productive business.
• Public transportation: It's becoming more common for cities to commission automated buses and subway systems for public transport. These systems can pick up and drop off passengers at designated stops while also having the safety capabilities that any other autonomous vehicle has.
• Tourism: A hotel, resort, or other tourism company might employ a fleet of buses that drive tourists around an area to show them the sights or drop them off at notable spots. By automating their tours, a business can save money and get people where they want to go more efficiently.

Robotic Process Automation
Robotic process automation (RPA) is a method by which software agents, also called bots, automate the performance of one or more tasks. The bots are not strictly robots in the sense that they act in a physical world, but rather they act with some degree of autonomy in a digital world.
In traditional RPA, the bot "watches" a user perform a task on their computer-for example, entering data in a database. The bot can then repeat this task much more quickly and efficiently, so the user's input is no longer needed. This does not necessarily exhibit AI since the bot is just repeating the same exact steps. However, RPA systems that do exhibit AI are able to learn from a user's input and then extrapolate that input into similar, but novel scenarios. For example, the bot may be able to enter data into a different database. Or, it is able to optimize the steps it takes to achieve the same goal. For example, the bot may have found a quicker way to connect to the database it needs to enter data into.
Some examples of AI-enabled RPA are as follows:
• Data entry and extraction: Data entry can be a tedious process, and humans are likely to make mistakes. RPA systems can quickly and accurately take raw data and transfer it to a more structured database. Likewise, they can extract data from a database that a data analyst needs, without that analyst needing to write complex querying code.
• Software testing: Testing is a crucial component of the software development lifecycle. Developers must ensure their code is robust and minimizes errors. However, there are many different kinds of tests, and running them manually can be slow and burdensome. RPA can start a number of test harnesses and respond to the results with some degree of intelligence. For example, the bot might run the test again for verification purposes, or it might tweak the parameters of the test slightly to see if the results are different.
• Customer service: Customer service reps need to carefully balance customer needs and business needs. It can be difficult to know what the appropriate response is, and when/should they offer a discount? A refund? Should they transfer the customer to another rep? In many cases, customer service reps struggle to follow a flow chart of responses. With RPA, a bot can determine the proper course of action based on various factors.
• Financial processes: The amount of transactions that banks and other financial institutions need to process is staggering. Some involve a degree of judgment or complexity, such as knowing whom it's appropriate to lend money to and how much. AI-enabled RPA systems can make these decisions with greater accuracy than a human. The bot doesn't necessarily replace a human entirely; a human likely reviews the bot's decisions and does or does not approve them.

Predictive Analytics for AI
Predictive analytics is a form of data analytics that uses data to forecast future behaviors. It provides estimates of the likelihood of a future outcome based on existing data, statistical models and algorithms, and probability theory to interpolate missing information or to extrapolate what might happen in the future. You can think of predictive analytics as providing the answer to the question, "What could happen?"
When it comes to using predictive analytics to optimize business processes, some examples include:
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic D

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 165
• Inventory levels: If an organization can predict how much inventory it will need in the future for one or more of its products, it will be able to increase or decrease current levels as needed to meet customer demand and business processing capability.
• Purchasing patterns: Just like maintaining the optimal inventory, it can be difficult to know the purchasing patterns to engage in for the business to run optimally. Predictive analytics can help you determine when it's the best time to buy something, whether due to pricing factors or external marketing factors. It can also help you pinpoint the optimal vendors to purchase from.
• Staffing needs: Some businesses need to plan and schedule their human workforce around certain events or other market factors. For example, a retail store might want to know what the optimal number of backroom workers is during a holiday or a sale so that the store can stay properly stocked and the workers can remain busy. Rather than guess, you can use predictive systems to anticipate the optimal level of staffing.
• Customer behavior: Customers, whether as individuals or as a collective, don't always appear to behave consistently. They may take advantage of a sale one day, but barely show up the next day. Of course, there are many factors that affect customer behavior that a human analyst can't always perceive. A predictive system, on the other hand, may be able to better anticipate customer behavior, giving you the chance to adapt your business strategy to optimize sales.

Supplier Relationship Management (SRM)
Supplier relationship management (SRM) is a process in which a business evaluates and attempts to strengthen the relationships it has with its suppliers, as well as better align those relationships with the business's overall goals and strategy. The purpose of SRM is to identify areas of improvement in a supplier relationship, whether that means finding new areas of value, minimizing cost, minimizing risk, etc. It is similar to custom relationship management (CRM), but focused on the role that suppliers play in the business.
AI can greatly improve on common SRM tasks, and even enable new approaches to building and maintaining supplier relationships. Some example applications include:
• Supplier segmentation: To make it easier to communicate with each supplier and manage other aspects of your relationships, you would typically segment suppliers according to one or more aspects. It might be based on the kind of product they supply, or where they are located, or your general attitude toward them. Using a technique like clustering, you may be able to more intelligently segment your suppliers according to multiple features instead of just a few obvious ones.
• Supplier evaluation: One major task of SRM is to evaluate your relationship with one or more suppliers. You need to make sure that such relationships are strong and still providing value to your business. An evaluation can include everything from "scoring" a supplier according to some metric, or describing them in more qualitative terms. In any case, an AI system might be able to score or classify each supplier more effectively than traditional tools.
• Supplier collaboration: It can be difficult to collaborate with suppliers that cross cultural, national, or language boundaries. Even suppliers in different sectors may operate in a way that is foreign to your business. AI can help your collaboration efforts. For example, you may be able to cluster your suppliers in such a way that you communicate differently to each cluster. Maybe one cluster prefers terse, results-driven discussions, whereas another cluster engages in open-ended discussions.
• Risk management: Risk is an important consideration in SRM just as it is in any business relationship. The business and the supplier both take on some degree of risk and must work together to minimize that risk. As discussed earlier, AI has many applications in risk management, and they apply to SRM as well. For example, an AI system may be able to identify areas of risk in the supplier and provide suggestions for how to address that risk in your own business.



Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic D

166 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Route Optimization
Route optimization is the process of finding the shortest path between two points. Traditional algorithms are able to identify this path in simple scenarios. But, it takes AI to account for the many factors that could impact how one gets from place to place, including weather conditions, road construction, hazards, traffic, rest stops, and so on.
Some examples of route optimization enhanced by AI include:
• Delivery: Whether a delivery vehicle is self-driving or not, an onboard navigation system powered by AI can quickly determine the fastest route from the business to the customer's home in order to get the product to the customer as fast as possible. It can also maximize fuel economy by having the vehicle avoid traffic-congested areas.
• Tourism: Tourism agencies can use AI-powered route optimization to help tourists get to their destinations faster. So, the tourists may have more time to see more sights.
• Warehouse navigation: Some industrial robots are able to move around an environment rather than just move a limb while stuck in place. These mobile robots may need to get from place to place within a warehouse or factory in order to retrieve or drop off parts or products. They'll need to be able to navigate various obstacles and get to their destination quickly; route optimization using AI can make this a reality.
• Video games and virtual worlds: Video games have been using rudimentary forms of AI for many years. Modern AI techniques can help developers create intelligent non-player characters that can move through the virtual world more efficiently, making them seem more lifelike and thus increasing the player's immersion.

























Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business | Topic D

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 167

Scenario
Consider the following discussion questions to help review your understanding of how AI is used to
optimize processes.

1. A recent internal review of your business processes confirms that your product inventory levels are not being managed efficiently. At any given time, there is either not enough inventory to meet customer demand, leading to customers being frustrated at out-of-stock notices; or, there is too much inventory taking up limited warehouse space, increasing storage costs and reducing cash flow from unsold product.
How might AI help address these inventory management challenges?




2. Another internal review of your business processes suggests that the assembly line in your warehouse is not running optimally. The human workers are not able to assemble your products quickly enough to meet business goals, no matter how skilled they are. There are frequent stoppages on the line, and there have been several safety issues as well.
How might AI help address assembly line challenges?

Lesson 7: Functions of AI in Business |

Implementing Business Requirements for AI
Lesson Time: 1 hour, 15 minutes




Lesson Introduction
Artificial intelligence (AI) is not a magic wand that you can wave to solve all of your problems. There are real costs involved, and many aspects you need to consider and plan for. In this lesson, you'll take a look at some of the requirements and challenges that AI poses, as well as how to develop strategies to address them.

Lesson Objectives
In this lesson, you will:
• Identify design requirements for implementing AI.
• Identify data requirements for implementing AI.
• Identify risk related to implementing AI.
• Develop a strategy for implementing AI.















Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

170 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
TOPIC A
Identify Design Requirements
Like any project, you should begin an AI project by identifying design requirements that will affect the rest of your team's efforts.

Use Case Identification
AI is transformative. Numerous organizations have already implemented AI solutions and are reporting their successes with it. It is easy to get tempted by the hype, focusing on the interesting possibilities of AI and looking for ways to force them into your business process, when instead you should focus on specific problems your organization needs to address, and then consider how AI might help you address the issues you have identified.
You'll need to identify use cases for AI that are relevant to your business. Some important factors to consider when doing this include:
• The problem(s) in your organization that need to be solved.
• Which problem(s) may or may not be solvable through traditional, non-AI technologies and processes.
• Existing processes that may be in place to address the problem(s).
• The overall goals of fixing the problem(s) (e.g., make operations more efficient and effective).
• Data that you have or can obtain that may pertain to the problem(s).
• Similar problem(s) that other organizations have faced and how they have solved them using AI.
In many cases, to address these factors, you'll need to consult with a wide variety of stakeholders. Such stakeholders can include technical personnel like AI practitioners who know more about the implementation of AI than you; but can also include non-technical audiences like customers who will be directly impacted by an AI solution.

The Value Proposition of AI
You also need to consider that AI is often an expensive business proposition. It can cost your organization a great deal of time, money, and effort to bring an AI solution to fruition. That's why you should try to quantify the value of any proposed AI solution.
For example, if you believe that a recommendation system will help lead to greater customer retention for your online store, how much more money in sales do you think you'll generate from these customers? How much money will you need to generate to justify the cost of designing, developing, testing, and implementing the recommendation system into production? Are there other ways the system could provide value to the business besides direct monetary increases (e.g., increased customer loyalty)? These are important questions to consider in any AI venture.

Prototype Assessment
In fields like AI, it is common to create a prototype to demonstrate to stakeholders what the solution will generally look like. So, if you plan on developing a system that can identify fraud in transactions, your team may build a preliminary classification model based on a sample of data, deploy that model into an ad hoc software environment, and then test the overall system by sending it some example transactions.
Presumably, you're one of the stakeholders they're demonstrating this prototype to. You're obviously not responsible for evaluating the technical details and inner workings of the prototype,

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic A

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 171
but you should be involved in assessing its design. You need to ask yourself (and your team) questions like:
• Is the overall design efficiently and effectively serving the needs of the target users?
• Will this system be scalable and able to support an increase in user demand?
• How much does the design rely on on-premises resources (e.g., servers) vs. cloud-based resources?
• Can the model be easily decoupled from the deployment environment in case it needs to be ported to another environment (e.g., as part of an upgrade)?
• Is the system designed in such a way that the model can be upgraded or improved over time using new training data?
• What will the maintenance effort and costs be for this system?


Note: Before a prototype, the business may also create a proof of concept (POC) to first determine whether or not the idea is even feasible.

TOPIC B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 173

Identify Data Requirements
Because AI is so data driven, the data itself becomes a crucial asset worthy of your special attention. In this topic, you'll address issues related to data that could impact your AI project.

Data Quality and AI
The quality of data can refer to a few things:
• Accuracy: How well it describes what it is meant to describe. Data is often recorded incorrectly due to human error, but is sometimes deliberately altered to be incorrect as part of an attack.
• Recentness: How up to date the data is. The data may have been perfectly accurate at one time, but if what it's describing undergoes significant change over time, then the data will be out of date.
• Completeness: Whether or not any is missing. Enough missing data can lead to low-quality AI models.
• Formatting: Whether or not it is in a format that requires a significant amount of processing. Data from different sources, each of which might have used different formatting standards, can be very time consuming to reconcile.
• Volume: The overall amount, whether this is measured by examples, features, or both. Generally, the more data you have, the better your AI models will be.
• Usability: How relevant it is to the problem at hand. Irrelevant data can be mixed in with relevant data, and it sometimes takes a skillful data analyst to spot the problem.
A deficiency in any one of these areas is going to negatively affect the data your team has to work with, which in turn will affect the quality of the models they create, which then has an effect on the overall AI solution the models are integrated into.
It's up to you and your team to determine which of these aspects is at greatest risk, as well as how to address that risk. For example, volume might not be as much of a concern if you're not developing a deep learning solution. Or, maybe completeness isn't as much of a factor if your team thinks it can ignore the missing data and still produce an effective model.
You might also take the opposite approach, where you identify which aspects of data quality are most important, then prioritize efforts to sustain them. For example, an AI system that makes life-and-death medical decisions should be trained from highly accurate data. You might double or triple check the data you use in an AI system against canonical data sources to ensure the data is kept accurate throughout the project.
Data Quality Models
There are many ways to represent the aspects of data quality beyond those just mentioned. You may want to consider reviewing an industry-recognized data quality model, such as ISO/IEC 25012: Software engineering - Software product Quality Requirements and Evaluation (SQuaRE) - Data quality model. This model includes 15 characteristics of data quality. You can learn more at https:// iso25000.com/index.php/en/iso-25000-standards/iso-25012.
There are also other data quality models out there that you may want to research on your own time. Some examples include the AHIMA Data Quality Management Model, the eCQI Quality Data Model, the UK Government Data Quality Framework, and so on.




Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic B

174 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
Data Control and AI
Data in the organization's possession must be tightly controlled, particularly if that data is of a sensitive or private nature. Some common data control techniques include:
• Acquisition: How is data collected and procured? Only certain sources or types of data may be allowed for use in an AI project. Untrusted, irrelevant, or sensitive data is either not acquired or is filtered out during acquisition.
• Storage: How and where is data stored? Data that is private in nature may, for legal reasons, need to be stored in certain locations and not others. Or, the organization may decide to segment the data for security or data management reasons, creating borders between physical or virtual storage locations. In either case, the organization is able to more tightly control data transfers across these borders.
• Ownership: Who "owns" data? In other words, who is ultimately responsible for the data. The data owner makes high-level decisions about how the data can be collected and used in an AI project, and is also accountable for the data should it be used improperly.
• Access permissions: What people or systems are able to read and use the data? Most AI practitioners and data scientists will need access, as do servers that ingest and process data for model building and prediction. Other stakeholders may also need to work with the data.
• Modification permissions: Aside from accessing the data, what people or systems need to be able to alter the data (e.g., to correct an inaccurate record)? You would typically restrict this level of access to only a few database administrators.
• Sharing permissions: What outside parties should have access to the data? Perhaps an external contractor is helping out with some facet of the AI project and they need access to the data. Their access should be controlled differently than internal personnel.
• Disposal: When and how should the data be disposed of? In many organizations, data that has served its purpose is disposed of to reduce the risk of it being compromised. You may also be legally obligated to dispose of certain data.
There may already be IT processes in place for generally controlling data in the organization that can be repurposed for data used in AI applications. However, because AI is such a heavily data-driven field, the use cases and needs regarding that data often require specialized planning. For example, an AI training system may need to continuously access a stream of user data, whereas in more traditional applications, the system may only need to access user data in an ad hoc fashion.

Data Security and AI
Security is a paramount concern when it comes to data of any sensitive or personal nature. This problem is compounded in a field like AI, where the organization is expected to consume and process massive amounts of sensitive and personal data. The more data you work with, the more you put at risk. Data is at risk of being:
• Copied by and disseminated to unauthorized users.
• Modified in unauthorized ways.
• Deleted without authorization.
• Made unavailable for use.
There are many ways to secure data. Controlling access to data, as mentioned earlier, is one major way. One guiding principle is the principle of least privilege, which states that a user should only be given enough permissions to be able to do their job, and no more than that. That way, if the user's account is compromised, the damage that account could do is minimal compared to if they had full access.
Another major data protection technique is encryption, which encodes data in such a way that only those in possession of the decryption key can read its actual contents. If an unauthorized user gains access to an encrypted database, they won't be able to do much with it if they don't have the key.
Encrypting data is important not only when it is stored in databases (at rest), but also when it is in transit over a network or currently in use by a server or other computer processing that data.
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 175
Ultimately, you must work closely with your IT team to develop a reasonable plan for securing the data that goes into your AI project.

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic B

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 177
TOPIC C
Identify Risks in Implementing AI
All business ventures, AI included, are subject to risk. However, AI has its own unique set of risks that you must identify and address as early in the project's lifecycle as you can.
Impact on Employees
One of the most pressing issues of introducing AI in the business is its effect on human labor. In particular, AI may displace jobs traditionally performed by humans on an unprecedented scale. This can include blue-collar jobs as well as white-collar jobs. The jobs most at risk are those that incorporate high amounts of routine and predictable behavior, especially behavior that a machine learning model can quickly learn with minimal amounts of training.
Naturally, your employees may feel anxiety (sometimes called technostress) as some of their job functions become more and more automated. This can lead to symptoms such as frustration, irritability, insomnia, headache, and more. Any one of these symptoms can negatively affect employees' productivity-for example, they may be more likely to commit errors or lose focus on the task at hand. They may also seek employment elsewhere if they feel their health is suffering.
Even if employees don't fear being replaced by AI, they may still struggle to adapt to it. They likely are used to following a specific set of processes that require their full input and perhaps some degree of creativity. AI can disrupt this workflow, making it seem it's "getting in the way" of the employee doing their job. Over time, employees will likely adapt to the AI as they would with any organizational change, but there is still a risk of lost productivity during that transitional period.

Operational Risks
AI is a disruptive force to the business-in a good way. But, just like employees need to adapt to AI, so too does the business. By implementing AI solutions, you necessarily take on a new set of risks, including the risk that the AI solution will:
• Outright fail.
• Not bring as much value to the business as the solution(s) it's replacing.
• Not be cost beneficial.
• Not be well integrated into other areas of the business.
• Not be easily updated and maintained.
Of course, not every implementation of an AI solution is going to be disruptive. But the more your business starts relying on AI to support its operations, the greater the risk. Your organization must therefore meet a certain level of readiness before you begin incorporating disruptive AI solutions. A change management approach can ensure that any disruptive AI solutions are incorporated into business operations in an efficient and effective way, minimizing the risks just mentioned and better preparing the organization to harness the power of AI.

Ethical Considerations
The ethics of AI involve thinking about how:
• Intelligent machines treat human beings.
• Intelligent machines act as moral agents.
• AI may cause harm to humans, whether physical, psychological, financial, or other types of harm.
As a business decision maker, you won't necessarily be asked to take on the deep philosophical issues involved with AI. However, you are still responsible for ensuring that your AI solutions
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic C

178 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
follow general ethical principles. In some organizations, ethics have actually become a standard part of corporate board reporting.
The following table describes five general principles in emerging and data-driven technologies, like AI, that your business should strive to uphold.

Privacy	Privacy focuses on protecting sensitive data about people from unauthorized or unwanted access. AI systems often collect an incredibly vast amount of information, and any business or individual in control of these AI systems effectively has control over the information. Users may not even be aware of the vast amount of data that is being collected about them and fed into AI systems. Likewise, the entities controlling this data may not be completely aware, either-especially if the AI systems are not closely monitored and managed.
Accountability	Accountability is all about ensuring people are held responsible for their actions. AI can make accountability a challenge, since decisions of great consequence are made by machines and not people. If a machine learning model makes a false cancer diagnosis, and the patient goes through an ordeal as a result, who is responsible for the mistake? The practitioners who trained the model? The project manager for the AI solution? The doctors who relied on the model's diagnosis? These questions are not always easy to answer, and unfortunately, many businesses do not even attempt to address them.

Transparency and explainability










Fairness and non-discrimination

Transparency is about enabling people to see into the inner workings of the technology and its effects on people and society. Explainability is about being able to truly understand and interpret how the technology works and why. The complex nature of AI can make supporting these principles a challenge. This is especially true when it comes to the complexities of deep learning. Some deep learning algorithms are considered black boxes, meaning it is difficult (or impossible) for a human practitioner to determine why the model made a certain decision. So, it is unreasonable to "trust" the model. If the decision the model makes is a bad one, then fixing the problem may not be plausible.
These principles ensure people are not treated improperly because of who they are. They are primarily undermined by bias, which is a major issue in AI. Machines process the data that is given to them, just as we expect them to. These machines might be very good at learning the data and drawing conclusions from it, but what of the data itself? The data is typically fed from a human, or is at least the product of some prior human guidance. This means the data has the potential to exhibit bias, or undue positive or negative attitudes applied to individuals, groups, or ideas. So, if the model is trained on biased data, it will produce biased results. This can lead to significant harm.



Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic C

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 179

Safety and security	Security, as you've seen, is all about protecting the
organization's assets. Just as AI can be used to enhance security countermeasures, so too can it be used to enhance attacks. Safety is related in that it is about protecting the human asset. In other words, minimizing the chance that people are physically harmed and that property (whether physical or virtual) is compromised. AI-powered industrial machinery is commonly thought of as a source of safety risk, but even virtual systems can put people at risk. For example, AI-powered traffic management systems can increase the rate of car crashes if they make poor decisions.

Operationalizing Ethical Principles
Recognizing ethical principles is one thing, but actually putting them into practice in the organization is another. There are many policies, procedures, tools, metrics, and other techniques that you can use to operationalize these principles. Applying encryption techniques to private user data is an example of operationalizing privacy. Using software tools that can identify what features an AI model considers most important to its predictions is an example of operationalizing explainability. There are many such examples.
AI Risk Management Framework
A valuable resource in addressing operational and ethical risks incurred by AI is the AI Risk Management Framework (RMF) published by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). The AI RMF outlines a process by which an organization can manage such risks. It does this by describing characteristics of trustworthy AI, such as AI that is valid, reliable, safe, fair, secure, and so on.
The AI RMF also offers four functions (Map, Measure, Manage, and Govern) that comprise the AI RMF Core, where each function enables certain outcomes and actions that are favorable toward managing AI risk (e.g., identifying risk is an outcome of the Measure function).
Lastly, the AI RMF encourages organizations to create profiles, which describe how the aforementioned characteristics, functions, and outcomes are applied to specific use cases, sectors, or industries. For example, a fair hiring profile could help an organization address fairness risks in an AI system that assists the hiring process.


Note: Learn more about the AI Risk Management Framework by visiting https:// www.nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework.
Legal and Regulatory Considerations
Given how new AI is in the world of business, not to mention how complex it is from both a technical and ethical standpoint, there are not yet many laws and regulations that govern its usage. However, as the technology matures and becomes more commonplace in people's lives, you will undoubtedly need to become more proactive about identifying what your business must comply with to stay in good legal standing.
There are a few examples of documents that may have some legal implications regarding the usage of AI. The following table lists four.




Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic C

180 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	

DoD AI Principles	In February 2020, the U.S. Department of Defense (DoD) adopted a set of principles after consulting with various AI experts across multiple industries and sectors. These principles are expected to be upheld in all facets of the military, including combat and non-combat roles.
The principles require that usage of AI is:
• Responsible
• Equitable
• Traceable
• Reliable
• Governable
For more information, see https://www.defense.gov/News/Releases/ Release/Article/2091996/dod-adopts-ethical-principles-for-artificial-intelligence/.

U.S. AI Bill of Rights

In October 2022, President Joe Biden unveiled a blueprint for a new AI Bill of Rights. This Bill of Rights is meant to enumerate five core principles that are useful in upholding the rights, opportunities, and needs of American citizens as they pertain to AI.
The principles involve:
• Safe and effective systems
• Algorithmic discrimination protections
• Data privacy
• Notice and explanation
• Human alternatives, consideration, and fallback
For more information, see https://www.whitehouse.gov/ostp/ai-bill-of-rights/.

EU AI Act	In 2021, the European Union proposed the Artificial Intelligence Act as a way to regulate the use of AI in the EU. As of December 2022, it has not yet been made into a law, but if it is, it will be the first law governing AI by any major regulatory body. Unlike the AI Bill of Rights, this act is not focused on the rights of individuals, but as a way to regulate the use of AI systems.
The regulation recognizes three major categories of AI risk:
• Unacceptable risk (e.g., government-run social credit scoring)
• High-risk systems (e.g., job applicant ranking)
• Low-risk systems (unregulated)
For more information, see https://artificialintelligenceact.eu/.

National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence

In 2018, India published its National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence, which provides guidance for how the country could adopt AI in a way that promotes social and economic growth as well as serve the greater good.
The strategy outlines three distinct components for AI adoption in India:
• Economic opportunities
• Social development and inclusive growth
• India as an "AI playground" or "garage" in which organizations can develop solutions for use in other economies
For more information, see https://indiaai.gov.in/research-reports/ national-strategy-for-artificial-intelligence.

Note: You may have the opportunity to consult with regulatory bodies as new AI regulations are developed. It's important to take a measured approach to regulation so that it will not have a significant negative impact on innovation and competitiveness in the AI space.

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic C

TOPIC D

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 183

Develop an AI Strategy
Unless you have a strategy for incorporating AI into your business operations, you'll be unable to truly harness the value of your work. In this last topic, you'll ensure that your AI projects fit within the overall mission of the business.

Alignment to Organizational Initiatives
As part of its overall strategy, your organization likely has one or more initiatives that are currently driving the business. The AI solutions you develop and implement must be considered alongside these initiatives, otherwise they risk being stranded without adequate support from decision makers. AI solutions that are isolated from strategic initiatives also risk being a hindrance to the business rather than a benefit.
Consider an initiative like digital transformation, in which the organization seeks major change through the use of digital technologies like cloud services, mobile devices, Internet of Things (IoT), and, of course, AI. The goals of such an initiative usually center on promoting innovation in the marketplace or improving the efficiency of business processes. AI is obviously an attractive option for accomplishing this. The organization should develop an AI solution not just as a way of solving a specific problem, but as a way of achieving digital transformation.
There are many other types of initiatives that hold relevance for AI. For example, your organization might have a data strategy that seeks to drive innovation by focusing on the possibilities that can be unlocked through data. As a data-driven technology, AI is a great candidate for supporting this initiative. Or, perhaps your organization has a general business growth strategy, where it wants to grow the business by some dollar amount over some period of time. You can fit an AI solution into this strategy by aligning it with those specific growth objectives, e.g., how can the AI solution help generate sales or reduce costs over the time period?
It's also important to recognize that AI must align with one or more existing strategies rather than being its own initiative disconnected from the rest of the business. This is particularly true regarding integration with IT strategies. For example, an autopilot AI system developed for an aircraft must be interoperable with other aircraft systems and must also have undergone rigorous safety and security tests in alignment with existing strategies, otherwise, it could prove disastrous.

Alignment to Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) Initiatives
Environmental, social, and governance (ESG) is a framework by which stakeholders can evaluate an organization's risks and opportunities regarding these three areas of concern. AI solutions have the potential to impact these areas in significant ways, so it is a good idea to align them with ESG initiatives whenever feasible.
There are several things to consider with each area of ESG as it pertains to AI.
• Environmental: The "E" is focused on how the organization impacts the environment in various ways, and how it is committed to sustainability. When it comes to AI, one of the most overarching environmental issues is power consumption. The hardware that AI solutions run on requires massive amounts of power. So, the organization could demonstrate to stakeholders that it is taking steps toward improving the efficiency of its power consumption.
• Social: The "S" is focused on how the organization has an effect on various social issues, and how it is committed to treating its employees, customers, partners, and communities fairly. As discussed earlier, AI solutions are subject to bias, which can lead to social harm. In addition to promoting fairness and non-discrimination, the organization could demonstrate to stakeholders
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic D

184 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	
that an AI solution is supporting the ideas of diversity, equity, and inclusion. When these ideas are considered during AI development and deployment, the solution is more likely to positively affect at-risk communities, achieve fair and just results, and generate participation from a variety of viewpoints.
• Governance: The "G" is focused on the organization's leadership and management, and how it is committed to the viability of the business. There are various concerns in governance, including upholding the rights of shareholders, conducting internal audits, and reviewing executive pay structures. One concern that is relevant to AI is the implementation of internal controls-in particular, data protection processes. The organization could demonstrate that leadership is taking user privacy and company security issues seriously during the development and deployment of AI solutions.

Organizational Resources
An AI project can be a significant undertaking for the organization, and will likely require resources that span many different departments and areas of expertise.
• Talent resources: As a field, AI is complex and technical. It requires skilled practitioners to successfully implement in any organization. You may wish to engage third-party contractors who specialize in AI, but don't discount your existing pool of talent. With the right training and hands-on experience, personnel like data analysts and software developers can become effective members of your AI development team. You also need to consider that AI solutions are about more than just direct development; you'll need IT personnel who are familiar with the intersection of development and operations (DevOps) to actually deploy an AI solution that can be accessed over a network.
• Data resources: As you're well aware by now, data is the main driver of any AI solution. It is perhaps the most important resource for the project. You need to account for how you'll acquire, process, and store data that may be useful to the project. Some data is open source and easily acquirable, whereas other data is closed source and requires licensing agreements and possibly fees. Other data may be internal to your organization, whether it's already available or waiting to be generated. Whatever the case may be, your strategy must incorporate data handling and management processes.
• Computing resources: Even if you plan on running every part of an AI project in the cloud, you must still be aware of what underlying computing resources are necessary to do so. This includes the CPU and GPU clusters used in training models; the storage devices used to maintain sources, backups, and copies of datasets; and the network bandwidth used in delivering predictions and other decisions to hundreds, thousands, or perhaps millions of consumers. You must consider these resources as flexible, able to scale up and scale down as demand rises and falls.
• Budget resources: AI projects can vary greatly in size and scope, and so can their costs. There are several common sources of cost that you should consider focusing on, including: the cost of acquiring software licenses used in developing AI solutions; the cost of deploying AI solutions as a service, whether in the cloud or using on-premises servers; and the cost of forward-looking activities like maintaining, improving, and scaling up solutions over their lifecycles. Also, the resources described previously (talent, data, and computing) naturally have costs associated with them. You must consider all of these factors as you budget for your AI projects.
• Stakeholders: As mentioned before, stakeholders can provide valuable assistance and unique perspectives when it comes to an AI project. Don't hesitate to solicit their opinions and requirements before, during, and at the end of a project. After all, stakeholders like users and employees will be the ones most directly affected by your AI solutions.

Competitive Considerations
AI can give an organization a great competitive edge in the marketplace. Products, services, and processes that are rooted in traditional technologies and practices are starting to feel outdated now, and in many cases, will soon become obsolete. Organizations that don't start seriously considering
Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Lesson 8: Implementing Business Requirements for AI | Topic D

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 185
how they can adopt AI could lose business to those that have been embracing AI for years. So, part of your business strategy should be to position AI as a force that drives your business to industry leadership, or, at the least, maintains your current position as an industry leader.
Some examples of how AI can boost an organization's competitiveness include:
• Improving the efficacy or usefulness of existing products and services that are common to the industry.
• Fostering innovation in a stagnant industry.
• Streamlining business processes so that an organization's operational margins are much better than its peers.
• Generating renewed interest from an industry's customer base, or attracting new customers to that base.
• Attracting new talent to the organization so that it can grow more rapidly.

ACTIVITY 1-1: Understanding Value, Insights, and Data

1. A) Identify new/incremental value: What elements of value would be important to produce year-round, high-quality fresh flowers for Greene Organix's distributors?
A: Answers may vary, but should include: cost, real-time availability, consistency and quality of product, freshness, unique varieties, year-round availability, longevity, and others.
2. B) Understand insights needed to support value: What information may be needed to drive down production costs so you can continue to deliver best-in-class flowers at improved competitive prices?
A: Answers will vary, but should focus on specific ways to drive down operational costs. Information required for this goal might include: investigating the feasibility of introducing additional automation into the operational process.
3. C) Data: What data do you need to collect to support these insights?
A: A local area network could be implemented through cabled connections, such as Ethernet, although a wireless network would make it easier to relocate sensors as needed.
4. Sensors: What useful information might an IoT device measure in this environment?
A: Answers may vary, although a greenhouse operator might be interested in CO2 levels, humidity, temperature, light exposure, soil moisture, nutrients concentration, airflow, plant imaging, and liquid-level detection.
Actuators: What actions might an IoT device need to perform in this environment?
A: Actuators in a greenhouse environment might include CO2 generators, air blowers and vents, irrigation pumps and valves, plenum or shade actuators, artificial lighting controls, heaters, and air conditioners.
Other sources: What external data might be of use to support the insights?
A: Answers may vary, but might include: weather data, material-supplier data, logistics data, inventory data, and commodities-market pricing information.

ACTIVITY 1-2: Selecting an IoT Infrastructure

1. A) What would be a typical IoT infrastructure setup for our greenhouses?
A: Answers will vary, but should include: Sensors, actuators, gateways, and connectivity between them; remote connectivity for our gateway to communicate to the cloud; a process to configure (add, delete, and move) sensors and actuators within our solution (provisioning and tracking); the ability to supply power to sensors, actuators, and gateways; a remote-user connectivity application; a cloud IoT platform to ingest all the data and a streaming analytics process to analyze the data; and access to external data sources (weather data, market commodities data, etc.) to merge with our collected data.
2. B) What are some constraints we may be dealing with within our greenhouse operation?
A: Answers will vary, but should include: The cost to implement and maintain (the incremental cost must be less than the incremental value created); security; remote locations of warehouses and extreme conditions where internal equipment may not be reliable; greenhouse sites may be too remote for Wi-Fi-based communications from the hub to the cloud (the solution might need to be cellular or satellite based); safety-related parameters that must be considered first, and possibly an extra layer of security for this data and control; any data that must be analyzed and processed locally to ensure rapid response and no reliance on remote network connection issues; and whether or not to limit the amount of data sent to the cloud (more data means more cost, both for transmission and for storage).

ACTIVITY 1-3: Identifying Potential Challenges of IoT

1. A) Challenges: What challenges might your organization encounter in implementing an IoT solution?
A: The answer depends on the nature of the proposed implementation and unique challenges within the organization. However, several themes are common, such as cost and possible disruption to current operations. Because IoT implementations may lead to automation and changes in job responsibilities, there may be challenges related to change management, such as getting buy-in, retraining staff, updating documentation, and so on.
2. B) Countermeasures and remediations: How might you address the challenges you identified?
A: Identifying what might go wrong is an important part of the design process. If you identify potential problems at the start of the project, you can design a solution that will prevent them from occurring. Various countermeasures and remediations to common IoT challenges are described in the next lesson.

ACTIVITY 2-1: Using IoT to Solve Problems

1. How might IoT help Best Elevators increase customer loyalty?
A: If IoT can provide improvements in all of the other problem areas, then presumably customer satisfaction will increase, and customers will be less likely to explore competitive options. Also, implementing a connected services strategy will improve the customer experience, and will provide customers with a sense that Best Elevators is a trusted partner in their daily operations, which should also contribute to increased customer loyalty.
2. How might IoT help Best Elevators improve service maintenance and predictive maintenance?
A: Data collection from sensors combined with analytics can be used to inform Best Elevators and their customers when maintenance is required. Early-warning data and remote diagnostics would enable the organization to dispatch technicians promptly, ensuring they are equipped with the necessary information, tools, and parts to restore the equipment to safe operating conditions quickly.
3. How might IoT help Best Elevators manage spare-parts inventory more efficiently?
A: IoT can be very helpful for keeping spare-part inventories in warehouses and service vehicles stocked appropriately, which is normally a notoriously difficult task to manage cost-effectively. On the supply side, inventory can be tracked using typical IoT devices such as RFID tags. On the demand side, elevator equipment can be monitored and analyzed using data analytics and Best Elevators' own domain expertise to predict future needs. Combining historical data of part failure rates with analytic algorithms, Best Elevators can optimize their part stocks in the warehouse and technicians' service vehicles, reducing inventory costs and improving repair time.
4. How might IoT help Best Elevators improve product design and technician training?
A: Once IoT systems are in place, the system will be able to collect and analyze usage patterns and maintenance requirements over time. This information can be used to improve product design and technician training.
5. How might IoT help Best Elevators improve uptime and field service efficiency?
A: The solutions described in the previous questions will help improve uptime and field service efficiency.
6. How might IoT help Best Elevators allocate scarce service technicians more efficiently?
A: If product design, elevator uptime, and field service efficiency are improved (as described in the previous two answers), then this will reduce the demand on service technicians. Improvements in technician training should make technicians more efficient and effective.
7. How might IoT help Best Elevators communicate more effectively with suppliers?
A: Parts distributors and value-added resellers will also see additional opportunities to leverage the insight garnered from the IoT connected-services solution. Best Elevator can share its experience and knowledge with its equipment manufacturers, creating a value-based competitive advantage in a market that is highly cost driven with pricing volatility.

ACTIVITY 2-2: Preparing for an IoT Initiative

1. What would need to be done next in this project?
A: Answers will vary depending on the development methodologies your organization follows. You've already initiated the project and developed a system concept. There may be more planning to do, and requirements have not been completely identified yet. So it seems that the project is in the late planning stage. The next phase would be identifying requirements. Typically, before delving into design and development, the team would translate the general concept into formal requirements so all project stakeholders can agree on specifically what the project should accomplish.
2. What sorts of change-management issues might arise as the company shifts from primarily selling physical goods to selling services?
A: Best Elevators is currently focused on manufacturing and maintenance operations. The new system will establish a new branch of operations focusing on software services. This will have some impact on staffing and organization structure. The payment model may change, as the company's orientation shifts from primarily selling physical goods to selling services. This will have numerous effects on the organization. You will also have some change-management issues with customers. While many customers will welcome the improvements Best Elevators is proposing, some will be suspicious and may resist becoming more dependent on Best Elevators. You'll need to ensure that customers understand how the improvements will benefit them and their tenants, and may even save them money on maintenance costs. Planning the technical aspects of an IoT project is complex enough, but to ensure the success of the project you should also carefully consider the impact on personnel, contractors, customers, business operations, and the financial bottom line.

ACTIVITY 3-1: Discussing Data Science and Related Technologies

1. Susan begins by asking you how you would define data science.
A: Data science is a discipline that involves accumulating and analyzing data so that you can extract value from the data. It also involves presenting the value of the data in a meaningful way that answers a business question or solves a problem.
2. You then spend a little time discussing the parts of data science. How would you describe the skills and knowledge required to perform data science?
A: Data science is a combination of mathematical skills, computing skills, and domain knowledge.
3. You mention to Susan that part of your duties at the Esker Road store includes reviewing shrinkage reports to determine the percentage of loss due to theft each month. What type of analytics is this activity?
A: This activity consists of reviewing data to determine past behavior, so this is an example of descriptive analytics.

Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Solutions

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 195
4. Susan asks if you are involved in setting up employee schedules for your store, and you agree that this is part of your responsibilities. What type of analytics is this most closely related to?
A: Scheduling staff includes an estimation of how many associates will be needed on certain days. In other words, you are predicting how busy the store will be and how many associates are needed to ensure that customers can be assisted promptly. So, this activity is a form of predictive analytics.
5. As you wrap up your session with Susan, she suggests that if GCE is truly going to embrace a data-driven culture, other technologies will probably be needed. What types of technology is she referring to?
A: Related concepts and technologies include artificial intelligence (AI), generative AI, machine learning (ML), deep learning (DL), Python or R programming, application programming interfaces (APIs), web scraping, and business intelligence (BI).

ACTIVITY 3-2: Identifying Data Science Opportunities from a Dataset (Optional)

2. What kind of information about each transaction is recorded in the dataset?
A: The dataset tracks the unique invoice ID of the transaction; the date and time of the transaction; the name, gender, and age of the customer; whether the customer is a member of the store's rewards club; the branch location the transaction took place at; and what category of item was purchased.
3. How might you use descriptive analytics with this dataset?
A: Answers may vary, but some examples include: drawing conclusions about the overall shopping behavior of customers; creating visualizations that compare shopping behaviors between the different branches; calculating statistics concerning different genders and age groups, like the age group that purchased the most clothing; and so on.
4. How might you use diagnostic analytics with this dataset?
A: Answers may vary, but some examples include: identifying why certain times of day or days of the week see a decline in sales; determining why a gender or age group is less likely to be a member of the rewards club; understanding why a certain branch location is lagging behind in sales of a specific product line; and so on.
5. How might you use predictive analytics with this dataset?
A: Answers may vary, but some examples include: determining what time of day someone of a certain age is likely to do their shopping; predicting what type of product someone will purchase based on their age, gender, and membership status; predicting days or weeks in which sales are likely to decline for future months; and so on.
6. How might you use prescriptive analytics with this dataset?
A: Answers may vary, but some examples include: proposing suggestions for changing store hours to better accommodate customer preferences; encouraging a different approach to advertising certain product lines, such as increasing the visibility of deals and promotions for those product lines; creating new membership-club perks to entice a certain gender or age group into joining; and so on.

ACTIVITY 6-1: Discussing the History of AI

1. Identify at least two events, people, or systems since the end of the second AI winter that have had a direct impact on the upward trajectory of AI.
A: Answers may vary. Some examples include: In 1997, Deep Blue defeated chess grandmaster Garry Kasparov in one of the first demonstrations of creative problem solving in AI. In 2004, the DARPA Grand Challenge kicked off, putting self-driving cars in the spotlight. In 2011, IBM's Watson defeated Ken Jennings and Brad Rutter at Jeopardy!, and Apple's Siri became the first virtual assistant with AI capabilities. In 2016, Google's DeepMind project demonstrated significant advances in general-purpose deep learning capabilities by defeating champion Lee Sedol at Go. Since 2020, systems like GPT-3 and DALL·E have given end users the ability to generate convincingly realistic text and images.

2. Identify at least two relatively recent technologies that have enabled AI to flourish in the business world, and briefly explain how they have done so.
A: Answers may vary, but there are essentially three recent advances that have paved the way for AI in business: cloud computing, general-purpose GPUs, and big data. Cloud computing has made it easier for even small businesses to access and manage the high infrastructure costs of AI systems without having to operate equipment locally. General-purpose GPUs have greatly accelerated the performance of AI systems due to their high memory bandwidth and ability to efficiently perform complex linear algebra calculations. Big data has increased the value proposition of AI systems since more data (in terms of volume, variety, etc.) tends to lead to better AI.

ACTIVITY 6-2: Discussing AI Concepts

1. Which technique, traditional machine learning or deep learning, would be most suitable to achieving this business objective? Why did you make this choice and not the other?
A: Answers may vary, but deep learning is almost certainly the better option. Deep learning models excel at processing large amounts of data, especially unstructured data like images. Deep learning models can extract the features in those images without anyone from your team needing to extract them manually, which would be extremely difficult if not impossible. Traditional machine learning would be better suited to simpler, more structured datasets where extracting features is either easy or has already been done for you.
2. Is this an example of supervised, unsupervised, or reinforcement learning? Explain why.
A: This is an example of unsupervised learning because there are no labels for the model to learn from. The model must instead determine common attributes from the customers' features and then place each customer into a cluster with other, similar customers.
