Solutions

202 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	

ACTIVITY 7-1: Improving User Experiences Using AI

1. What AI system(s) could help solve this problem to improve the customer experience, and how?
A: Answers may vary, but there are two types of systems that could actually help: chatbots and agent assistance systems. Chatbots could help by funneling any customer service requests that come in through a website or app into an automated system that can act as a first point of contact, answering basic and common questions by processing customers' text-based messages. This will free up agents to address more complex requests. On the other hand, agent assistance systems can work alongside call center agents to more efficiently address customer issues. For example, the assistant can quickly search and retrieve troubleshooting steps for a specific problem faster than the human agent. This will reduce time spent on each call and will make the call more productive, leading to more satisfied customers.
2. How can an AI-driven recommendation system provide more effective product suggestions to customers?
A: Answers may vary. An AI-driven recommendation engine can learn many different factors that can help it make an informed decision about what to suggest. For example, it might build a profile of the customer based on their location, age, gender, spending habits, etc., then use that profile to discover what other people with similar profiles tend to buy. The AI system can also make more intelligent decisions based on the nature of the products themselves, such as recommending a product that "matches" the product the customer purchased-like recommending a monitor to a person who just bought a desktop computer.

ACTIVITY 7-2: Segmenting Audiences Using AI

1. What machine learning technique can help you place customers into different groups for the purposes of market segmentation? Provide a basic explanation for how the technique works.
A: Answers may vary, but unsupervised learning, specifically clustering, is the most prominent method for segmenting customers using machine learning. It finds commonalities in the attributes of each customer and then places each customer alongside other customers that they most closely resemble.
2. How can a machine learning model that makes predictions be used to enhance customer segmentation?
A: Answers may vary. A prediction system can use techniques like classification or regression to predict some aspect of each customer that isn't already known. This aspect may play an important role in determining which segment to assign the customer to. The assignment might be directly based on the prediction result, or it might be incorporated into a clustering model that considers the prediction result alongside many other features when segmenting customers.

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 203

ACTIVITY 7-3: Securing Assets Using AI

1. How might AI help the security team focus on the real incidents that threaten the organization?
A: Answers may vary, but AI-powered systems can help the security team classify, rank, and prioritize threats and alerts so that only the most pressing matters are brought to their attention. This will reduce the chance that the team acts on false positives, enabling them to spend more time addressing the important issues.
2. How can AI improve your IT asset management process so that these kinds of incidents are avoided as much as possible?
A: Answers may vary. First, an AI system that can receive monitoring data from the device, particularly factors that indicate the device's health, can be used to predict the failure of that device. That way, the IT team can order replacements ahead of the actual failure, ensuring there is no downtime when it actually happens. Second, instead of having a team member spend time researching devices, an AI system can help them select a proper replacement to narrow down their choices, making the procurement process quicker and easier.

ACTIVITY 7-4: Optimizing Processes Using AI

1. How might AI help address these inventory management challenges?
A: Answers may vary, but predictive analytics can help the business estimate how much inventory will be needed to meet customer demand in the future, along with considering factors like storage costs and a reduction in cash flow. The business can use these predictions to determine whether they need to increase or decrease their current inventory levels. This will lead to optimal inventory levels, keeping costs low and customers happy.
2. How might AI help address assembly line challenges?
A: Answers may vary, but industrial robots were created to address issues like this. They can assemble and disassemble parts much more quickly and accurately than any human. They also help minimize safety issues, as some assembly line work can be dangerous if not done properly.

ACTIVITY 8-1: Identifying Design Requirements for AI

1. What are some important factors you should consider when identifying use cases for AI in your organization?
A: Answers will vary. Perhaps the first and most important step is to start by identifying the problems you need to solve, then determining how AI can solve them. This is better than the opposite approach, in which you just try to force AI into your business without good reason. You should also consider what your overall goals are, and how AI can help you meet those goals. Another important factor is data-how can existing data be used, or how can new data be obtained, that is relevant to the problem? You should also consider stakeholders and what input they have that could guide your decisions.




Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Solutions

204 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	

2. What questions should you ask yourself or your team to help you assess the design of this prototype?
A: Answers will vary. One important question is, will the cloud servers be scalable? In other words, if demand for predictions increases significantly, will the cloud environment be able to meet this demand? Another question is, will the AI engineers have the ability to decouple the model from the cloud environment in case some major change needs to be made? Likewise, is the model able to be easily trained with new data so that its effectiveness can be improved over time? There are many such questions and concerns that are important to assessing the design of a prototype.

ACTIVITY 8-2: Identifying Data Requirements for AI

1. What are some data quality concerns that you have regarding this project?
A: Answers will vary. One concern is how long or how many resources it will take to actually combine all of the data. If the data is from disparate sources, then consolidating all of it into a single, consistent format may prove very challenging. Another potential concern is the recentness of the data. Could the data about the customers' demographics or purchasing activity be outdated? It might no longer align with current information if enough time has passed.
2. What are some security risks that could affect the data being used in this project?
A: Answers will vary. The data could be at risk of being intercepted when in transit over the VPN, though it likely has some encryption in place. However, once the data is on the employees' laptops at a remote location, it is open to many more risks, including theft of the data if the user's home network isn't secured, or if the laptop is stolen from a public location. The security of the master dataset within the enterprise network should also be considered. Is it properly encrypted? If not, then the security of the data is at risk from the very beginning. Is it protected against availability issues? If not, then the employees won't be able to work on the data remotely.

ACTIVITY 8-3: Identifying Risks in Implementing AI

1. What are some of the business risks involved in adopting this AI solution, from both an employee and an operational standpoint?
A: Answers will vary. From an employee standpoint, the HR team members may be pleased that they no longer need to spend time screening applicants, but they also may be wary of their jobs being totally replaced by automation. Their job performance may suffer as a result, or they may seek other employment. They may also need time to learn how to use or interpret the results of the job applicant solution, which can lead to lost productivity in the short term. From an operational standpoint, there is the risk that the job applicant solution will do no better than the HR personnel at filtering applicants-or it may perform worse. Even if it's faster, it may not be worth the cost of development and maintenance. Speaking of maintenance, you have to consider the risk of the solution becoming outdated as the job landscape changes, and without a clear plan for updating the solution, it could end up being more of a hindrance than a benefit in the long term.


Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

Solutions

ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210) | 205
2. What are some ethical concerns you would have regarding this solution?
A: Answers will vary. The model behind the solution may need to be trained on large volumes of
personal data about job applicants. It will also likely need to take even more of that data as input when it's making its filtering decisions. If the personal data is compromised at any point in the process, it will lead to major privacy issues (and possibly legal action). The model might also be found in violation of fairness and non-discrimination principles by exhibiting bias in its filtering practices. It might filter out perfectly qualified applicants based on protected attributes or attributes not relevant to the job, like race, gender, nationality, religion, etc.

ACTIVITY 8-4: Developing an AI Strategy

1. How might the AI job applicant screener impact each of the three aspects of the ESG framework? How might you address these impacts in your AI strategy?
A: Answers will vary. The AI system doesn't seem like it'd have much of a direct impact on the environment. However, the resources used to create, operate, and maintain that solution likely will. So, you might impress upon stakeholders your commitment to running the system as efficiently as possible. The social impact can be significant since the screener is directly impacting people's lives. Its fairness may be called into question, so, as part of the AI strategy, you might make a commitment to ensuring it is free of major bias. On the governance side of things, the organization's leadership must commit to implementing user privacy and data protection controls. Otherwise, job applicants' personal data could be leaked or otherwise mishandled.
2. What are some organizational resources that you need to consider as part of your AI strategy? Why are these resources relevant to the job applicant screener?
A: Answers will vary. In any AI project, talent is a big resource concern. The organization must employ or contract skilled AI practitioners who know how to turn data into an effective AI model. Likewise, data itself is a major resource that the organization needs to consider. Where will it be getting data that it can use to train the job applicant screener? Are there historical datasets of job applicants that the model can be trained on? Are there enough examples, or enough features? Are the datasets labeled? These are questions that the AI strategy needs to answer. Another resource to consider has to do with stakeholders. There are potentially many stakeholders in a project like this, but of particular note are HR personnel and the department managers who perform interviews of candidates screened by the AI system. They likely have valuable input as to what constitutes an acceptable job candidate at each stage of the process.

Glossary




agent assistance
Automation technology that helps human
agents in a call center do their jobs more efficiently and effectively.
aggregation
The process of gathering and summarizing
data in a summarized report.
AGI
(artificial general intelligence) A speculative
type of AI in which machines have a similar level of cognitive abilities as humans. Also called general AI or strong AI.
AI
(artificial intelligence) The ability of
machines to exhibit human-like intelligence, as well as the scientific discipline concerned with this idea.
algorithm
A mathematical procedure or technique
that, when converted into code, enables a computer to identify patterns, make predictions, or perform tasks without explicit instructions.
API
(application programming interface) A
component that connects disparate pieces of software together.
audience segmentation
The process of dividing individuals into
groups based on common characteristics.

autonomous vehicle
A vehicle with self-driving capabilities.
BI
(business intelligence) Strategies and
technologies used by enterprises for the analysis of business data, which transforms that data into useful information.
big data
Collections of data that are so large and
complex that they require advanced tools to process and analyze them.
black box
The property by which a machine learning
model's decisions are difficult to understand or explain.
C2
(command and control) A set of
organizational and technical attributes and processes that use human, physical, and information resources to solve problems and accomplish missions to achieve the goals of an organization or enterprise.
causation
A relationship between two variables
where a change in one variable causes a change in the other variable.
chatbot
A type of AI system that uses natural
language processing (NLP) to engage in real-time, text-based chatting with a human user.



Licensed For Use Only By: Peter Hassen Sep 18 2026 5:08AM

208 | ETBIZ (Exam ETZ-210)	


classification
A type of supervised learning in which a
model attempts to place a data example into a class or category.
cloud computing
An approach to providing computing
services and other resources over a network of distributed servers and other infrastructure.
cloud security
The application of cybersecurity
techniques and principles to cloud platforms, the infrastructures they are built on, and the resources they use.
clustering
A type of unsupervised learning in which a
model groups data examples together that exhibit similar attributes.
COM
(Component Object Model) A standard for
software components introduced by Microsoft in 1993 that provides a language-neutral way of implementing objects that can be used in environments different from the one in which they were created, even across machine boundaries, and that provides the basis for several other Microsoft technologies and frameworks.
computational statistical inference
See statistical inference.
computer vision
The discipline that involves computers
understanding images and video at a high level.
connected services
A service relationship between a customer
and a product vendor made possible through network connections provided in the vendor's product.
constrained device
A product that contains a processor whose
capabilities are limited by low power-usage requirements, low memory, low processor speed, compact size, and so forth.

correlation
A statistical association between two or
more variables.
CPAP device
(continuous positive airway pressure
device) A machine that facilitates a patient's healthy breathing while sleeping.
CPS
(cyber-physical system) A system in which
software (cyber) technologies model and control physical production equipment and processes.
crowdsourcing
The act of outsourcing work and services
to a group of people, such as an online community, who aren't internal employees of the organization.
customer churn
The tendency of customers to cancel
subscriptions to a service, or otherwise cease their relationship with a business.
CX
(customer experience) The overall
perception of a company based on customers' interactions with that company.
data analytics
The science of examining raw data to
derive insights and conclusions that help businesses make better decisions or to verify or disprove existing models or theories.
data augmentation
The process of generating new data points
from existing data.
data cleaning
The manipulation of data to remove
inconsistencies and address missing data points.
data dashboard
A visualization tool that provides users
with a way to view key performance indicators and status for an enterprise.

A collection of moral principles that govern
how organizations gather, protect, and use data.

A set of policies and procedures that govern
how data is collected, stored, and used.

A collection of raw data that is retained in its

The ability to read, analyze, communicate, and

The process of pulling actionable insight out of

The practice of communicating meaningful

digital transformation
A business initiative in which the organization
seeks major change through the use of digital technologies.

(deep learning) A type of machine learning that
makes complex decisions using multiple layers of information.

The buying, selling, and moving of bulk energy
(electricity and natural gas) from where it is produced to where it is needed.

(environmental, social, and governance) A

In the field of machine learning, an individual
instance of data in a training set.
feature

property of an example in a training set.
feature engineering
The process of using domain knowledge,
mathematics, and statistics to manipulate a dataset to facilitate the training of an ML model.

GPU
(graphics processing unit) A computer chip
typically used to process and output visual data like graphics and images, but which also optimizes the performance of mathematical calculations used in AI.
ground truth
See label.
hypothesis testing
A statistical inference process that enables you
to prove (or disprove) an assumption.

(Industrial Internet of Things) The use of IoT
technologies to extend the traditional capabilities of industrial control systems. Also called Industry 4.0.
industrial robot
A robot used in warehouses and manufacturing
facilities to automate dangerous or difficult processes.
IoT
(Internet of Things) A network of physical
devices, vehicles, home appliances, and other items embedded with electronics, software, sensors, actuators, and connectivity that enables these things to connect and exchange data and integrate the physical world with

model target

model training

model validation
The process of feeding a set of testing data

narrow AI

Glossary

OPC
(Open Platform Communications) An
industrial standard for interprocess communication initially based on Microsoft's standards for distributed computing.
open data
Data that is freely available for use and that can
be shared or built on by anyone for any reason.
operationalization
The process of moving data and models out of
a sandbox and into a live production environment.
OT
(operational technology) Hardware and
software used to detect or cause changes in physical processes through direct monitoring and control of physical devices such as valves and pumps, typically used for industrial control.
PAC
(programmable automation controller) A type
of industrial automation controller that combines attributes of a programmable logic controller and a personal computer.
PLC
(programmable logic controller) An industrial
digital computer that has been ruggedized and adapted for the control of manufacturing processes, such as assembly lines, robotic devices, or any activity that requires high reliability control, ease of programming, and process fault diagnosis.
predictive analytics
A form of data analytics that uses data to
forecast future behaviors.
predictive maintenance
Techniques that help determine the condition
of in-service equipment in order to predict when maintenance should be performed, potentially saving costs over routine or time-based preventive maintenance because tasks are performed only when warranted.
preprocessing
A series of activities that improve the quality of
data to facilitate in-depth analysis.

prescriptive analytics
A form of data analytics that uses data to
predict future outcomes and then offer recommendations for actions based on those predictions.
prescriptive maintenance
An approach to predictive maintenance in
which required maintenance is predicted by systems, and a course of action is prescribed. Essentially, it is preventive maintenance with built-in intelligence.
preventive maintenance
A statistical approach to periodic maintenance
that requires maintenance at standard intervals, determined by the frequency with which parts have failed in the past.
principle of least privilege
A security principle that states a user should
only have enough permissions to be able to do their job effectively, and no more than that.
proactive maintenance
Any approach that attempts to schedule
maintenance before a problem occurs, hopefully preventing a run-to-failure (RTF) situation.
proprietary data
Data that is owned and controlled by an
individual, organization, or group and that might be protected by copyright, patent, or trade-secret legislation.
recommendation system
A system that suggests items, services, and
other things of interest to users.
recommender system
A form of machine learning that suggests
potential items of interest based on a customer's profile and search history.
regression
A type of supervised learning in which a model
attempts to predict some numerical value.
regression analysis
A statistical inference method that estimates
the relationship between a dependent variable and one or more independent variables.
